arXiv:2604.07016v1 [cs.LG] 08 Apr 2026

Predictive Representations for
Skill Transfer in Reinforcement Learning

Ruben Vereecken Affiliation: Computer Science, Imperial College, UK    Luke Dickens Affiliation: 
Information Studies, UCL, UK    Alessandra Russo Affiliation: Computer Science, Imperial College,
UK

Abstract

A key challenge in scaling up Reinforcement Learning is generalizing learned behaviour. Without the
ability to carry forward acquired knowledge an agent is doomed to learn each task from scratch. In
this paper we develop a new formalism for transfer by virtue of state abstraction. Based on
task-independent, compact observations (outcomes) of the environment, we introduce
Outcome-Predictive State Representations (OPSRs), agent-centered and task-independent abstractions
that are made up of predictions of outcomes. We show formally and empirically that they have the
potential for optimal but limited transfer, then overcome this trade-off by introducing OPSR-based
skills, i.e. abstract actions (based on options) that can be reused between tasks as a result of
state abstraction. In a series of empirical studies, we learn OPSR-based skills from demonstrations
and show how they speed up learning considerably in entirely new and unseen tasks without any
pre-processing. We believe that the framework introduced in this work is a promising step towards
transfer in RL in general, and towards transfer through combining state and action abstraction
specifically.

Research conducted: September 2018 – June 2021.
This manuscript represents the work as of June 2021.

1 Introduction

One core feature humans seem to share is the tendency to think of a task in terms of subtasks; the
act of navigating through a door may require taking out a key, putting it to a lock and turning a
handle before moving through the doorway. However, at no point while reading this would you think
of the minute motor actions involved. There appear to be levels of abstraction, higher-level more
abstract actions such as unlocking a door, described in terms of taking out a key, putting it in
the lock etc, while intermediate level actions such as taking out a key might decompose into
lower-level more primitive actions such as muscle contractions. It is this phenomenon that gave
rise to hierarchical reinforcement learning (HRL), a popular area of present-day research concerned
with the decomposition of tasks and learning of subtasks.

Not only are humans adept at decomposing tasks, there is also the notion that we can reuse abstract
behavior across a variety of settings where subtasks can be said to reoccur. The act of unlocking a
door is, intuitively, mostly independent of which door is being unlocked or the shape of the key,
and the differences can be thought of as variations on a common theme. In this paper, we refer to
abstract actions in this context as skills, and suggest that this reusability of behavior, i.e.
this transfer of skills, is the core advantage of action abstraction. This notion it not new and
others have discussed action abstraction as a basis for transfer in RL (Konidaris et al., 2012;
Barto et al., 2013), yet there is one obstacle standing in the way of skill transfer: a behavior
learnt in one particular task’s state space can not readily be used in a different one. There is a
fundamental need for state abstraction. The skill of opening a door is independent of the color of
the door or the shape of the key; it acts on the abstract concepts key and door and ignores
specifics. In a recent opinion piece, Konidaris advocates for joint state and action abstraction (
Konidaris, 2019). In this paper we show that state and action abstraction naturally go hand in
hand.

This paper presents a new formalism for thinking about how to transfer learned behavior from one
MDP to another (Section 2). This formalizes the concept of state abstraction and both describes how
to transfer a policy between MDPs by means of the transfer cover, i.e. the overlap between state
abstractions, and the impact on performance. Based on outcomes (Section 3.1), environmental
features that are reward-relevant and task-agnostic, we introduce outcome equivalent state
abstractions which can be constructed from predictions of outcomes (Section 3.2). We define
plannable MDPs as MDPs where reactive, state-dependent policies, can not outperform non-reactive
plans, and prove that outcome equivalent state abstractions allow optimal transfer between
plannable MDPs (Section 3.3).

To overcome the limiting factor of transfer cover, we introduce the more practical
Outcome-Predictive State Representations (OPSR) and integrate these ideas with action abstraction
in the form of skills (Section 4). We show how these skills can be learned in a fully automated way
such that combined, they achieve full transfer between tasks by virtue of the OPSR state
abstraction. In a collection of empirical studies, we both learn these skills from demonstrations
and show that they speed up learning considerably in entirely new and unseen tasks with zero
pre-processing (Section 5). Section 6 describes the work on state abstractions and HRL that form
the rich backdrop for this work, even detailing a cognitive motivation for OPSRs. We conclude that
the framework introduced in this work constitutes a promising step towards a general approach for
transfer (Section 7).

2 General Framework

This section builds the theoretical framework of transfer based on state abstraction that we use as
a foundation to build and assess a new type of state abstraction in Section 3. We develop
formalisms for comparing state abstractions, detail how transfer can be conducted in both the
single- and multi-task setting, and describe in each setting how state abstractions impact
performance following transfer.

2.1 Markov Decision Processes and Problems

Reinforcement Learning is concerned with sequential decision-making in an environment which is
perceived through states and rewards. It is conventional to describe a Reinforcement Learning task
as a Markov Decision Process.

Definition 2.1 (Markov Decision Process).

A Markov Decision Process (MDP) is defined as a 4-tuple M≐⟨𝒮,𝒜,p,r⟩{M}\mathrel{\doteq}\langle\
mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p,r\rangle, where 𝒮\mathcal{S} is the discrete set of states and 𝒜\mathcal
{A} the set of actions. The MDP dynamics are described by the two functions pp and rr:

  • •
   
    We write the probability to transition between states s,s′∈𝒮s,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
    following action a∈𝒜a\in\mathcal{A} as

     p⁡(s′|s,a)≐Pr⁡(St+1=s′|St=s,At=a).p(s^{\prime}|s,a)\mathrel{\doteq}\Pr(S_{t+1}=s^{\prime}|S_
     {t}=s,A_{t}=a).

    This function is called the transition dynamics of the MDP.

  • •
   
    We write the expected reward for a transition (s,a,s′)∈𝒮×𝒜×𝒮(s,a,s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\
    times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S} as

     r(s,a,s′)≐𝐸[Rt+1∣St=s,At=a,St+1=s′].r(s,a,s^{\prime})\mathrel{\doteq}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\
     mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[R_{t+1}\mid S_{t}=s,A_{t}=a,S_{t+1}=s^{\prime}}}\right].

    We will also rely on the notion of expected reward for an action in a given state:

     r(s,a)≐𝐸[Rt+1∣St=s,At=a].r(s,a)\mathrel{\doteq}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\
     left[R_{t+1}\mid S_{t}=s,A_{t}=a}}\right].

    Both functions can be referred to as the reward dynamics of the MDP and which one is actually
    in use can be inferred from the context.

The goal in Reinforcement Learning is then to find an optimal behaviour in an MDP. A behavior is
represented by a policy π⁡(a|s)=Pr⁡(At=a|St=s)\pi(a|s)=\Pr(A_{t}=a|S_{t}=s). Optimality is defined
in terms of expected long-term accumulated reward. To properly define this, we need to instantiate
a Markov Decision Problem. A Markov Decision Problem is simply a Markov Decision Process extended
with a discount factor γ\gamma, so that we can define the long-term accumulated reward as a
discounted sum of rewards.

 Gt≐∑k=1∞γk−1​Rt+k.G_{t}\mathrel{\doteq}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\gamma^{k-1}R_{t+k}.  (1)

We can now define the value of a policy for a given state as the expected accumulated reward from
that state.

 vπ(s)≐𝐸[Gt∣St=s]v_{\pi}(s)\mathrel{\doteq}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[G_   (2)
 {t}\mid S_{t}=s}}\right]

Where necessary to avoid ambiguity, we include the MDP M{M} in the value function and instead write
vπ​(s,M)v_{\pi}(s;{M}). The task in Reinforcement Learning now consists of finding a policy π\pi
that maximizes the quantity (2) for some state ss, which is usually a fixed start state.

One last note on notation. We sometimes rely on partially applied functions, i.e. functions where
some of the parameters are given and some are left out. For example, π(⋅|s)\pi(\cdot|s) is the
policy for state ss. This allows us to say for example that a policy has the same distribution over
actions for two different states ss and s′s^{\prime}, denoted π(⋅|s)=π(⋅|s′)\pi(\cdot|s)=\pi(\cdot|
s^{\prime}). We often rely on comparing functions in this fashion. For another example, vπ​(⋅,M)
≥vπ′​(⋅,M)v_{\pi}(\cdot;{M})\geq v_{\pi^{\prime}}(\cdot;{M}) denotes that the value function of π\
pi is greater or equal than that of π′\pi^{\prime} for all states in its domain, that being some
state set 𝒮\mathcal{S}. If the MDP M{M} is clear from context, this can again be simplified to
vπ≥vπ′v_{\pi}\geq v_{\pi^{\prime}}. Such comparisons are only defined when the functions are
defined over the same domain.

2.2 Representation and Abstraction

2.2.1 State representation

Representations – and in particular, state representations – are inextricably linked with problem
descriptions in contemporary literature. For our purposes, it is important to define explicitly
what a representation is in order to critically compare different representation approaches and to
develop our own. A representation stands in for the real thing. Put another way, it is a proxy for
the raw, real-world thing that it represents. It follows that a state representation is a
description for states, standing in for the underlying environment’s configurations. Whether
manually defined or somehow learned, state representations help enumerate large or continuous state
sets. Not only that, state representations can also provide a common language to describe tasks
that we might think of as related. For example, we can represent the states of all Gridworlds as
coordinate tuples (see Example 2.1).

Definition 2.2 (state representation).

A state representation ρ\rho is a function mapping from a set of raw states 𝒳\mathcal{X} to a state
representation set 𝒮ρ\mathcal{S}_{\rho}

 ρ:𝒳↦𝒮ρ\rho:\mathcal{X}\mapsto{\mathcal{S}_{\rho}}

We term 𝒮ρ{\mathcal{S}_{\rho}} the state set associated with ρ\rho. Given a raw state x∈𝒳x\in\
mathcal{X}, we term s≐ρ⁡(x)s\mathrel{\doteq}\rho(x) the representation of xx, overloading the
meaning of the term representation to mean both the function and a raw state’s image. Where the
relation with the representation ρ\rho is clear, we drop the subscript from 𝒮ρ\mathcal{S}_{\rho}
and simply write 𝒮\mathcal{S}.

Since we cannot access the raw states 𝒳\mathcal{X} of the true environment being modelled, state
representations are always used when defining a MDP, albeit usually implicitly. It is also possible
to define further state representations, possibly based on the existing ones.

It has become common to make use of the notion of structure of a state set, for example by
employing a similar policy in states that look similar. To facilitate this, state spaces can be
used in place of state sets. A state space is simply a state set with added structure (such as a
metric). The type of space is dependent on the problem at hand and is often assumed implicitly.
Metric spaces are common as they employ a distance metric which can be used to measure the distance
between states. For example, if raw Gridworld states were reprsented by coordinate tuples,
neighboring states would be closer than non-neighboring states under the Euclidean metric on the
coordinate space.

Definition 2.3 (state space).

Given a state representation ρ:𝒳↦𝒮\rho:\mathcal{X}\mapsto{\mathcal{S}}, if 𝒮\mathcal{S} is a space,
i.e. a set with some added structure, then we term it the state space associated with ρ\rho and
denote it with a non-caligraphic SS.

A state representation can be as simple as a scheme for enumerating states such that each has a
unique identifier which one can use to point at (to represent) that state. We refer to a
rudimentary representation that uniquely identifies each state as a ground representation.

Definition 2.4 (ground representation).

A state representation ρ0:𝒳↦𝒮ρ0\rho_{0}:\mathcal{X}\mapsto{\mathcal{S}_{\rho_{0}}} is a ground
representation if and only if ρ0\rho_{0} is injective, that is, raw states that are distinct have
distinct representations.

Example 2.1.

Take the simple Gridworld in Figure 1. There are 5 states corresponding to the location of the
agent. In order to refer to these states we can employ a representation. A simple enumeration is
feasible: we can number the states in ascending order according to nearness to the goal location,
with s1s_{1} denoting the state depicted in Figure 1 (with the agent located in the upper left
cell) and s5s_{5} denoting the state where the agent occupies the goal location. This is a valid
ground representation, as is every permutation — we could have numbered them the other way around.


Figure 1: A simple Gridworld labelled to construct a representation. The shaded area is
inaccessible to the agent and the goal cell with positive reward is indicated by the green circle.

We have already established that multiple ground representations are possible for our simple
Gridworld example, yet we could have followed another representation scheme altogether. Since the
agent’s locations are conveniently arranged in a grid, we could label each state with a (x,y)(x,y)
coordinate pair. Employing the numbering in Figure 1, we can identify the following 5 states:
(1,1),(1,2),(2,2),(3,2),(3,1)(1,1),(1,2),(2,2),(3,2),(3,1) – another ground representation. We can
summarise its signature as ρ0:𝒮↦{1,2,3}×{1,2}\rho_{0}:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\{1,2,3\}\times\{1,2\}.
Some caution should be exercised with this description though: the codomain of ρ0\rho_{0} does not
span the entire image. That is, the state (2,1)(2,1) does not actually exist. Likewise we could
have written ρ0:𝒮↦N2\rho_{0}:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\mathbb{N}^{2}, with the same caution.

Given that states are laid out in a grid, it is tempting to use a metric that describes similarity
or ‘closeness’ of grid cells. Using the Euclidean metric for example, (1,1)(1,1) is closer to (1,3)
(1,3) than it is to (2,3)(2,3). Similarity between states under some metric is sometimes used as a
basis to transfer behavior. It is far from obvious however that states that are close together in
this example would benefit from similar behavior. Note, for instance, that (2,3)(2,3) is reachable
from (1,1)(1,1) in fewer (non-diagonal) steps than is (1,3)(1,3) in spite of its greater Euclidean
distance. (Now that I added this paragraph, I need to add a citation of an example where similarity
between states is used for transfer). ∎

Many representations with distinct images are ground representations, yet they all fulfill the same
purpose of uniquely labelling states. For instance, we could have easily devised a different
numbering scheme in the above example. Isomorphism neatly captures this notion of similarity and
allows us to focus only on meaningful differences between representations.

Definition 2.5 (isomorphic ground state representations).

Given two ground state representations ρα:𝒳↦𝒮α\rho^{\alpha}:\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
and ρβ:𝒳↦𝒮β\rho^{\beta}:\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathcal{S}_{\beta}, if there exists a bijection between
𝒮α\mathcal{S}_{\alpha} and 𝒮β\mathcal{S}_{\beta} (i.e. an isomorphism), then we say that ρα\rho_{\
alpha} and ρβ\rho_{\beta} are isomorphic or equal up to an isomorphism:

 ρα≅ρβ\rho^{\alpha}\mathrel{\cong}\rho^{\beta}

It follows that every bijective ground representation is isomorphic with the identity state
representation.

2.2.2 State Abstraction

Abstractions themselves are representations of a sort. Yet abstractions are more than that: they
aim to focus on just the relevant information and ignore the irrelevant. In the context of
transfer, good abstractions should focus on relevant commonalities between tasks. We see
abstractions throughout this paper as a molding of distinctions, masking those that are less
relevant. To remove all that distinguishes two things is to abstract away from them and equate them
as a result.

A state abstraction is a special form of state representation which is potentially non-injective:
it supports the mapping of distinct raw states onto the same abstract state.

Definition 2.6 (state abstraction).

Given an MDP M=⟨𝒮,𝒜,p,r⟩{M}=\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p,r\rangle, a state abstraction ϕ:𝒮↦Φ\
phi:~\mathcal{S}~\mapsto~\Phi maps from a state representation set 𝒮\mathcal{S} to an abstract
state set Φ\Phi. We term ϕ⁡(s)∈Φ\phi(s)\in\Phi the abstract state corresponding to the ground state
s∈𝒮s\in\mathcal{S}. The inverse abstraction ϕ−1:Φ↦P⁡(𝒮)\phi^{-1}:\Phi\mapsto\mathbb{P}{(\mathcal
{S})} maps an abstract state ϕ⁡(s)∈Φ\phi(s)\in\Phi onto the set of corresponding ground states
{s′∈𝒮|ϕ⁡(s′)=ϕ⁡(s)}\set{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}{\phi(s^{\prime})=\phi(s)} and where P⁡(X)\mathbb
{P}(X) denotes the powerset of XX.

Since there are many possible state abstractions for any given state set, it is useful to again
rely on the notion of isomorphism between state abstractions to describe state abstractions that
are the same in every relevant aspect.

Definition 2.7 (isomorphic state abstractions).

Let ϕα:𝒮↦Φα{\phi_{\alpha}}:\mathcal{S}\mapsto{\Phi_{\alpha}} and ϕβ:𝒮↦Φβ{\phi_{\beta}}:\mathcal{S}\
mapsto{\Phi_{\beta}} be two state abstractions. We say that ϕα{\phi_{\alpha}} and ϕβ{\phi_{\beta}}
are isomorphic or equal up to an isomorphism, denoted ϕα≅ϕβ{\phi_{\alpha}}\mathrel{\cong}{\phi_{\
beta}}, if all sets of ground states that correspond to the abstract states of ϕα{\phi_{\alpha}}
are the same for ϕβ{\phi_{\beta}}. That is, if {ϕα−1​(ϕ¯α)|ϕ¯α∈Φα}={ϕβ−1​(ϕ¯β)|ϕ¯β∈Φβ}\Set{{\phi_{\
alpha}^{-1}}(\bar{\phi}_{\alpha})}{\bar{\phi}_{\alpha}\in{\Phi_{\alpha}}}=\Set{{\phi_{\beta}^{-1}}
(\bar{\phi}_{\beta})}{\bar{\phi}_{\beta}\in{\Phi_{\beta}}}.

2.2.3 Loss of the Markov Property.

It is tempting to see a state abstraction as inducing a new decision process based on the abstract
state set. This can however be far from straightforward. It is possible that this abstract decision
process no longer maintains the Markov property.

Example 2.2.


(a)


(b)


Figure 2: On the left hand side is depicted an MDP with 5 states and the action set {a,b}\{a,b\}.
Arcs between states denote deterministic transitions following the labelled action, receiving the
annotated reward rr. An abstraction is shown as grouping together states. The resulting abstract
states are depicted on the right.

Figure 2(a) depicts an MDP to which a state abstraction is applied. This simple state abstraction ϕ
\phi groups states 2​a2a and 2​b2b together: ϕ⁡(2​a)=ϕ⁡(2​b)=ϕ¯2\phi(2a)=\phi(2b)=\bar{\phi}_{2}.
The other states remain ungrouped under the abstraction, i.e., each is mapped to a different
abstract state. Figure 2(b) shows the resulting abstract states, along with transitions labelled
with actions in an attempt at fidelity to the original MDP. There is a problem describing the
resulting abstract MDP however: it is impossible to express the transition probabilities from ϕ¯2\
bar{\phi}_{2} unambiguously in such a way that it accurately reflects the transition probabilities
of the ground MDP. The result of an action from ϕ¯2\bar{\phi}_{2} depends on the action that was
taken before (at ϕ¯1\bar{\phi}_{1}). This is because ϕ¯2\bar{\phi}_{2} is the abstraction of the
ground states 2​a2a and 2​b2b. It is no longer possible to distinguish between 2​a2a and 2​b2b, yet
they lead into different states: 33 and 44 respectively, with abstract states ϕ¯3\bar{\phi}_{3} and
ϕ¯4\bar{\phi}_{4} respectively. As a result, it is not clear whether a certain action from ϕ¯2\bar
{\phi}_{2} will lead to ϕ¯3\bar{\phi}_{3} or ϕ¯4\bar{\phi}_{4}. Not without knowing what preceded.
In short, the Markov property is violated.

Consider for example p⁡(ϕ¯3∣ϕ¯2,a)p(\bar{\phi}_{3}\mid\bar{\phi}_{2},a) which has two candidates.
The resulting state of action aa in ϕ¯2\bar{\phi}_{2} is either ϕ¯3\bar{\phi}_{3} or ϕ¯4\bar{\phi}_
{4} (in which case it is not ϕ¯3\bar{\phi}_{3}). The candidates depend on different histories over
actions:

              Pr⁡(Φ¯t+1=ϕ¯3∣Φ¯t=ϕ¯2,At=a,Φ¯t−1=ϕ¯1,At−1=a)=1\displaystyle\Pr(\bar{\Phi}_{t+1}=\bar
              {\phi}_{3}\mid\bar{\Phi}_{t}=\bar{\phi}_{2},A_{t}=a,\bar{\Phi}_{t-1}=\bar{\phi}_
              {1},A_{t-1}=a)=1
 ≠\           Pr⁡(Φ¯t+1=ϕ¯3∣Φ¯t=ϕ¯2,At=a,Φ¯t−1=ϕ¯1,At−1=b)=0\displaystyle\Pr(\bar{\Phi}_{t+1}=\bar
 displaystyle {\phi}_{3}\mid\bar{\Phi}_{t}=\bar{\phi}_{2},A_{t}=a,\bar{\Phi}_{t-1}=\bar{\phi}_
 \neq\;       {1},A_{t-1}=b)=0

That is, in order to know the outcome of action aa from ϕ¯2\bar{\phi}_{2}, it is necessary to know
which action was taken from state ϕ¯1\bar{\phi}_{1}. Similarly, it has also become impossible to
describe the expected reward function r⁡(ϕ¯2,a)r(\bar{\phi}_{2},a):

              𝐸[Rt+1∣Φ¯t=ϕ¯2,At=a,Φ¯t−1=ϕ¯1,At−1=a]=0\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\
              mathclose{{\left[R_{t+1}\mid\bar{\Phi}_{t}=\bar{\phi}_{2},A_{t}=a,\bar{\Phi}_{t-1}=\
              bar{\phi}_{1},A_{t-1}=a}}\right]=0
 ≠\           𝐸[Rt+1∣Φ¯t=ϕ¯2,At=a,Φ¯t−1=ϕ¯1,At−1=b]=1\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\
 displaystyle mathclose{{\left[R_{t+1}\mid\bar{\Phi}_{t}=\bar{\phi}_{2},A_{t}=a,\bar{\Phi}_{t-1}=\
 \neq\;       bar{\phi}_{1},A_{t-1}=b}}\right]=1

Both transition and reward dynamics have become dependent on the history, making it impossible to
construct an abstract Markov Decision Process from this state abstraction that is faithful to the
original MDP. ∎

As the example shows, a state abstraction does not necessarily lead to an obvious choice for an
abstract MDP. Instead, it results in a range of possible abstract MDPs, each a candidate that we
can learn a policy for. We will delve into ways of assessing different state abstractions and
abstract MDPs after we define some basic machinery.

Definition 2.8 (abstract MDP).

Given an MDP M=⟨𝒮,𝒜,p,r⟩{M}=\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p,r\rangle and state abstraction ϕ:𝒮↦Φ\
phi:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\Phi, we say that Mϕ=⟨Φ,𝒜,pϕ,rϕ⟩{M}_{\phi}=\langle\Phi,\mathcal{A},p_{\
phi},r_{\phi}\rangle is an abstract MDP for M{M} and ϕ\phi if and only if there exists a function
wϕ:𝒮↦[0,1]w_{\phi}:\mathcal{S}\mapsto[0,1] such that for all ϕ¯,ϕ¯′∈Φ\bar{\phi},\bar{\phi}^{\prime}
\in\Phi, it holds that ∑s∈ϕ¯wϕ​(s)=1\sum_{s\in\bar{\phi}}w_{\phi}(s)=1, and

 pϕ​(ϕ¯′|ϕ¯,a)\displaystyle p_ =∑s∈ϕ−1​(ϕ¯)∑s′∈ϕ−1​(ϕ¯′)wϕ​(s)​p​(s′|s,a)\displaystyle=\sum_{s
 {\phi}(\bar{\phi}^{\prime}|\  \in{\phi^{-1}}(\bar{\phi})}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in{\phi^{-1}}(\bar   (3)
 bar{\phi},a)                  {\phi}^{\prime})}w_{\phi}(s)p(s^{\prime}|s,a)
 rϕ​(ϕ¯,a)\displaystyle r_{\   =∑s∈ϕ−1​(ϕ¯)wϕ​(s)​r​(s,a)\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in{\phi^{-1}}(\  (4)
 phi}(\bar{\phi},a)            bar{\phi})}w_{\phi}(s)r(s,a)

By reuse of notation we may write the set of abstract MDPs for M{M} and ϕ\phi as ϕ⁡(M)\phi({M}),
such that Mϕ∈ϕ⁡(M){M}_{\phi}\in\phi({M}) and ϕ−1​(Mϕ)=M\phi^{-1}({M}_{\phi})={M}.

As a result of the freedom permitted by wϕ​(s)w_{\phi}(s), many abstract MDPs are possible for a
particular MDP and state abstraction. The abstract transition and reward dynamics are constructed
as weighted averages over the original transition and reward dynamics. The weighting wϕ​(s)w_{\phi}
(s) is the mechanism through which a new valid MDP is created, albeit only an approximation of the
original. The cost of state abstraction is a loss in fidelity to the original MDP. In this paper we
will discuss particular classes of state abstractions that result in abstract MDPs where the
freedom permitted by wϕw_{\phi} does not play a role and that are particularly useful for transfer.

2.2.4 Comparing Abstractions

State abstractions operating on the same state set can be compared to one another. The first state
abstraction property that allows comparison is coarseness (or conversely fineness), where one state
abstraction is finer than another if it makes more distinctions between states. The following
definition of coarseness is adapted from the work of Li et al. (2006) to take into account
isomorphism.

Definition 2.9 (coarseness).

Let ϕ1:𝒮↦Φ\phi_{1}:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\Phi and ϕ2:𝒮↦Φ′\phi_{2}:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\Phi^{\prime} be
two state abstractions. We say that ϕ1\phi_{1} is finer than ϕ2\phi_{2}, or equivalently that ϕ2\
phi_{2} is coarser than ϕ1\phi_{1}, written ϕ1⪰ϕ​2\phi_{1}\succeq\phi 2, if all states that are
equivalent under ϕ1\phi_{1} are also equivalent under ϕ2\phi_{2}. Formally:

 ϕ1⪰ϕ2iff∀s,s′∈𝒮:ϕ1​(s)=ϕ1​(s′)⇒ϕ2​(s)=ϕ2​(s′)\phi_{1}\succeq\phi_{2}\quad\text{iff}\quad\forall
 s,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}:\phi_{1}(s)=\phi_{1}(s^{\prime})\Rightarrow\phi_{2}(s)=\phi_{2}(s^{\
 prime})

We say strictly finer (≻\succ) or strictly coarser (≺\prec) if in addition ϕ1\phi_{1} and ϕ2\phi_
{2} are not isomorphic. We also refer to the coarseness of an abstraction as its expressivity; an
abstraction that is finer is more expressive.

The coarser relation imposes a partial ordering over a set of state abstractions ΘM\Theta_{M} for
an MDP M{M}. If there is no state abstraction ϕ′∈ΘM\phi^{\prime}\in\Theta_{M} that is finer
(coarser) than ϕ∈ΘM\phi\in\Theta_{M}, we say that ϕ\phi is a maximally fine (maximally coarse)
state abstraction in ΘM\Theta_{M}. If a state abstraction ϕ∈ΘM\phi\in\Theta_{M} is finer (coarser)
than all other state abstractions ΘM\Theta_{M}, we say that ϕ\phi is finest (coarsest). The finest
abstraction for ΘM\Theta_{M} is isomorphic with the identity state abstraction. The coarsest
abstraction for ΘM\Theta_{M}, on the other hand, maps every state onto a single abstract state,
equating all states and making no distinctions. It follows that there is only one isomorphically
distinct finest and coarsest abstraction for any MDP.

2.3 Transfer Through State Abstraction

The power of state abstractions becomes apparent when multiple ground states are mapped to the same
abstract state. When a behavior is learned for a single abstract state, it can apply automatically
for all ground states for this abstract state. It now becomes useful to see a state abstraction as
inducing an equivalence relation, where two states are equivalent when they share an abstraction.
An equivalence relation E⊆𝒮×𝒮E\subseteq\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{S} is a binary relation that is
reflexive, symmetric and transitive. If a state pair (s,s′)(s,s^{\prime}) is in EE, we say that ss
and s′s^{\prime} are equivalent. We employ the shorthand E⁡(s,s′)E(s,s^{\prime}) to denote (s,s′)∈E
(s,s^{\prime})\in E. The view of state abstractions as inducing equivalence relations is due to
Givan et al. (2003), the language and what follows is our own.

Definition 2.10 (abstraction-induced equivalence relation).

A state abstraction ϕ\phi induces an equivalence relation EϕE_{\phi} over a state set. If Eϕ​(s,s′)
E_{\phi}(s,s^{\prime}) we write s=ϕs′s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi}}{{=}}s^{\prime}. For states
s,s′∈𝒮s,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S} this relation is defined as

 s=ϕs′⇔ϕ⁡(s)=ϕ⁡(s′)s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi}}{{=}}s^{\prime}\iff\phi(s)=\phi(s^{\prime})  (5)

If s=ϕs′s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi}}{{=}}s^{\prime} we say that ss is equivalent to s′s^{\prime}
under the abstraction ϕ\phi.

An equivalence relation in turn results in a partitioning of the state set. States that share the
same abstraction belong to the same equivalence class. For an equivalence relation induced by a
state abstraction ϕ\phi, we can denote the equivalence class of a ground state ss under ϕ\phi as
[s]ϕ≐{s′∈𝒮|s=ϕs′}[s]_{\phi}\mathrel{\doteq}\Set{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}{s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\
phi}}{{=}}s^{\prime}}. For convenience of notation we overload ϕ⁡(s)\phi(s), so far used to denote
an abstract state, to also denote the equivalence class of ss under ϕ\phi, i.e. ϕ⁡(s)≐[s]ϕ\phi(s)\
mathrel{\doteq}[s]_{\phi}.

An equivalence relation EE on 𝒮\mathcal{S} induces a partition P={[s1],…,[sm]}P=\Set{[s_{1}],\dots,
[s_{m}]}. For an abstraction-induced equivalence relation we can write the partition as Φ={ϕ¯|ϕ¯≐ϕ⁡
(s)​∀s∈𝒮}\Phi=\Set{\bar{\phi}}{\bar{\phi}\mathrel{\doteq}\phi(s)\;\forall s\in\mathcal{S}}. In
addition, we have ⋃ϕ¯=𝒮\bigcup_{\bar{\phi}}=\mathcal{S} and ⋂ϕ¯=∅\bigcap_{\bar{\phi}}=\varnothing.
It follows that if there is at least one equivalence class with more than one member, i.e. if two
states share an abstraction, then the partition will be smaller than the original state set.

2.3.1 Single-Task Transfer.

The view of a state abstraction as inducing an equivalence relation between ground states is useful
because it gives us a way to transfer behavior learned for an abstract state to all ground states
in the corresponding equivalence class. Where before behavior had to be learned for each ground
state separately, now we only need to learn a policy for the abstract states. Since the abstract
MDP has fewer states to contend with, it can be easier to learn than the ground MDP.

If we have an MDP M{M} and a state abstraction ϕ\phi which induces the abstract MDP Mϕ{M}_{\phi},
then we can learn a policy πϕ:𝒮ϕ↦𝒫⁡(𝒜)\pi_{\phi}:\mathcal{S}_{\phi}\mapsto\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})
for this abstract MDP. To transfer this abstract policy to the ground task, we make use of a
process called policy derivation.

Definition 2.11 (derived policy).

Let M=⟨𝒮,𝒜,p,r⟩{M}=\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p,r\rangle be an MDP, let ϕ:𝒮↦Φ\phi:\mathcal{S}\
mapsto\Phi a state abstraction, and let Mϕ=⟨Φ,𝒜,pϕ,rϕ⟩{M}_{\phi}=\langle\Phi,\mathcal{A},p_{\
phi},r_{\phi}\rangle be an abstract MDP constructed from M{M} and ϕ\phi. Given an abstract policy
πϕ:Φ↦𝒫⁡(𝒜)\pi_{\phi}:\Phi\mapsto\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A}), we define the policy derived from πϕ\pi_
{\phi} as δ:𝒮↦𝒫⁡(𝒜)\delta:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A}) s.t. ∀s∈𝒮,a∈𝒜\forall s\in\
mathcal{S},a\in\mathcal{A}:

 δ⁡(a∣s)=πϕ​(a∣ϕ⁡(s))\delta(a\mid s)=\pi_{\phi}(a\mid\phi(s))

Note that we use the symbol δ\delta to denote a derived policy rather than the customary π\pi to
suggest that it is derived from an abstract policy.

Let Πϕ\Pi_{\phi} be the set of all abstract policies πϕ:Φ↦𝒫⁡(𝒜)\pi_{\phi}:\Phi\mapsto\mathcal{P}(\
mathcal{A}). We write the set of derived policies for state abstraction ϕ\phi and MDP M{M} as

 Πδ​(ϕ,M)≐{δ|δ⁡(s,a)=πϕ​(ϕ⁡(s),a)∀πϕ∈Πϕ}\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,{M})\mathrel{\doteq}\Set{\delta}{\delta
 (s,a)=\pi_{\phi}(\phi(s),a)\quad\forall\pi_{\phi}\in\Pi_{\phi}}

If M{M} is clear from the context, we also refer to Πδ​(ϕ,M)\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,{M}) as the derived
policies of ϕ\phi.

Policy derivation gives us a simple construction to translate an abstract policy to a policy for a
ground MDP. It is interesting to investigate what manner of ground policies can be derived given a
particular state abstraction, even before considering any abstract MDPs. As we shall see shortly,
state abstraction is restrictive. Only the most trivial of state abstractions allow access to the
whole set of ground policies by means of derivation. This means that different state abstractions
will result in different sets of derived policies. Some sets of derived policies might not even
include the optimal policy because of restriction by the state abstraction. We start with one
straightforward result that describes how state abstraction limits possible derived policies. After
that we will consider whether this limitation is harmful to the end goal: attaining good policies.

When a state abstraction assigns two ground states to the same abstract state, any abstract policy
can no longer distinguish between those two states as it only sees the combined abstract state. As
a result, any derived policy is also unable to distinguish between these two states, effectively
decreasing controllability or control granularity. Increased control is only possible by
distinguishing between states.

Proposition 2.1.

Let M=⟨𝒮,𝒜,p,r⟩{M}=\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p,r\rangle be an MDP and let ϕ:𝒮↦Φ\phi:\mathcal
{S}\mapsto\Phi be a state abstraction that maps two states s,t∈𝒮s,t\in\mathcal{S} to the same
equivalence class ϕ¯\bar{\phi}. Then all derived policies for ϕ\phi must have the same
distributions over actions for both ss and tt. Formally, ∀s,t∈𝒮:\forall s,t\in\mathcal{S}:

 ϕ(s)=ϕ(t)⇔∀δ∈Πδ(ϕ,M):δ(⋅∣s)=δ(⋅∣t)\phi(s)=\phi(t)\;\Leftrightarrow\;\forall\delta\in\Pi_{\     (6)
 delta}(\phi,{M}):\delta(\cdot\mid s)=\delta(\cdot\mid t)

For the proof see Appendix A on page Proof.

Since different state abstractions result in different restrictions on possible derived policies,
the level of control or control granularity is a useful property to characterise and compare
between state abstractions.

Definition 2.12 (control granularity).

Let M=⟨𝒮,𝒜,p,r⟩{M}=\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p,r\rangle be an MDP and let ϕ:𝒮↦Φ\phi:\mathcal
{S}\mapsto\Phi and ϕ′:𝒮↦Φ′\phi^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\Phi^{\prime} be two state abstractions.
We say that ϕ\phi is more control granular than ϕ′\phi^{\prime} if all derived policies contained
in Πδ​(ϕ′,M)\Pi_{\delta}(\phi^{\prime},{M}) are also contained in Πδ​(ϕ′,M)\Pi_{\delta}(\phi^{\
prime},{M}). Formally:

 Πδ​(ϕ′,M)⊆Πδ​(ϕ,M)⟺∀δ′∈Πδ​(ϕ′,M):∃δ∈Πδ​(ϕ,M):δ=δ′\Pi_{\delta}(\phi^{\prime},{M})\subseteq\Pi_{\
 delta}(\phi,{M})\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\forall\delta^{\prime}\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi^{\prime},{M}):\
 exists\delta\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,{M}):\delta=\delta^{\prime}

If in addition we have that Πδ​(ϕ,M)≠Πδ​(ϕ′,M)\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,{M})\neq\Pi_{\delta}(\phi^{\prime},
{M}), we say that ϕ\phi is strictly more control granular than ϕ′\phi^{\prime}.

State abstraction control granularity sounds a lot like state abstraction fineness or coarseness.
It turns out both properties are different sides of the same coin. Coarser state abstractions give
up more control; finer state abstractions have more control, are more control granular. The reason
we introduced the secondary notion of control granularity is that it is more amenable to the
context of control that we are faced with.

Proposition 2.2 (control granularity–coarseness trade-off).

Let M=⟨𝒮,𝒜,p,r⟩{M}=\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p,r\rangle be an MDP and let ϕ:𝒮↦Φ\phi:\mathcal
{S}\mapsto\Phi and ϕ′:𝒮↦Φ′\phi^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\Phi^{\prime} be two state abstractions.
Then ϕ\phi is finer than ϕ\phi if and only if ϕ\phi is more control granular than ϕ′\phi^{\prime}.
Conversely, if there exists a derived policy for ϕ\phi that cannot be derived for ϕ′\phi^{\prime}
then ϕ\phi must be finer, meaning that there must be two states s,t∈𝒮s,t\in\mathcal{S} that are
mapped to different abstract states by ϕ\phi but to a single abstract state by ϕ′\phi^{\prime}. 
Formally:

 ϕ⪰ϕ′⟺Πδ​(ϕ,M)⊇Πδ​(ϕ′,M)\begin{gathered}\phi\succeq\phi^{\prime}\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\Pi_{\   (7)
 delta}(\phi,{M})\supseteq\Pi_{\delta}(\phi^{\prime},{M})\end{gathered}

For the proof see Appendix A on page Proof.

Potential for transfer through state abstraction and policy derivation does not necessarily mean
that it is beneficial – the resulting derived policy is not necessarily a good one. For example, a
policy derived from an optimal abstract policy is not necessarily optimal in the ground MDP. Now
that we know that the set of derived policies Πδ​(ϕ,M)\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,{M}) is a subset of the set
of all ground policies, it pays to look at the value functions of those derived policies. This will
tell us what solution qualities are excluded by a state abstraction, impossible to be attained
regardless of the learning algorithm that might eventually be used, constrained as it must be by
the control granularity permitted by the state abstraction. What this will come down to eventually
is the question of how a state abstraction’s potential for transfer and the potential solution
quality permitted by that state abstraction are related.

Consider the value functions associated with the derived policies Πδ​(ϕ,𝒜)\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,\
mathcal{A}). These value functions constitute a partial order without the guaranteed existence of a
greatest element as there can be multiple policies that are optimal for the set of derived
policies. Still, this set of value functions can be useful as we can compare sets of value
functions of derived policies between state abstractions. If one state abstraction allows a derived
policy that trumps all derived policies of another state abstraction, then that state abstraction
is potentially better.

Definition 2.13 (transfer value).

Let M=⟨𝒮,𝒜,p,r,γ⟩{M}=\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p,r,\gamma\rangle be an MDP and let ϕ:𝒮↦Φ\phi:\
mathcal{S}\mapsto\Phi and ϕ′:𝒮↦Φ′\phi^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\Phi^{\prime} be two state
abstractions. We say that ϕ\phi has greater transfer value for M{M} than ϕ′\phi^{\prime}, denoted
vϕ​(M)≥vϕ′​(M)v_{\phi}({M})\geq v_{\phi^{\prime}}({M}), if there exists a derived policy for ϕ\phi
with a value function greater than or equal all derived policies’ value functions for ϕ′\phi^{\
prime}. Formally:

 vϕ​(M)≥vϕ′​(M)⟺∃δ∈Πδ​(ϕ,M):∀δ′∈Πδ​(ϕ′,M):vδ​(⋅,M)≥vδ′​(⋅,M)v_{\phi}({M})\geq v_{\phi^{\prime}}
 ({M})\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\exists\delta\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,{M}):\forall\delta^{\prime}\in\Pi_
 {\delta}(\phi^{\prime},{M}):v_{\delta}(\cdot;{M})\geq v_{\delta^{\prime}}(\cdot;{M})

We say that ϕ\phi has strictly greater transfer value than ϕ′\phi^{\prime}, written vϕ​(M)>vϕ′​(M)
v_{\phi}({M})>v_{\phi}^{\prime}({M}), if there is a derived policy in Πδ​(ϕ,M)\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,
{M}) that has a strictly greater value function than any derived policy in Πδ​(ϕ′,M)\Pi_{\delta}(\
phi^{\prime},{M}).

We should add that comparing transfer values can be conservative for some applications as the above
definition requires value dominance at all states. In practice there is often a set of start states
for which a higher value is far more important than for other states. In those scenarios, transfer
values could be compared for a subset of states rather than for all states. In this paper we limit
ourselves to comparing values for all states but limiting this comparison to a subset of states is
a straightforward extension.

The set of all possible ground policies may contain multiple optimal policies but they will all
have the same greatest value function. The same is not necessarily true for the set of derived
policies. Because policy derivation restricts the space of available policies, the optimal policy
might no longer be available which will be reflected in the transfer value of that state
abstraction. Instead of a single optimal derived policy, it is possible to have multiple derived
policies with maximal value, all suboptimal, all with value functions that are incomparable
(neither greater nor less). Since transfer value is defined in terms of value functions, it is also
possible for one state abstraction’s transfer value to not be comparable to that of another state
abstraction.

In effect, we now have two partial orderings over state abstractions: coarseness and control
granularity on one side and transfer value on the other. The two sides are of course related. To
describe how they relate, it is instructive to understand when one state abstraction has a higher
transfer value than another. It is only possible for a state abstraction to have a strictly higher
transfer value than another if it allows for more control where the other does not (and for that
improved control to support a greater value at one or more states). If two states are grouped
together under an abstraction that an optimal policy would distinguish between, the value of the
best available policy under this abstraction suffers and this is reflected in the transfer value of
this abstraction. This leads to the following Theorem.

Theorem 2.3 (transfer value–control trade-off).

Let ϕ:𝒮↦Φ\phi:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\Phi and ϕ′:𝒮↦Φ′\phi^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\Phi^{\prime} be
two state abstractions such that ϕ\phi has strictly higher transfer value than ϕ′\phi^{\prime} for
some MDP M≐⟨𝒮,𝒜,p,r⟩,γ{M}\mathrel{\doteq}\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p,r\rangle,\gamma. Then
there must exist two states s,t∈𝒮s,t\in\mathcal{S} such that ϕ′\phi^{\prime} does not distinguish
between them while ϕ\phi does, and there exists a derived policy for ϕ\phi that takes advantage of
this distinction by controlling ss and tt differently in a way that no derived policy for ϕ′\phi^{\
prime} can do. By giving up representational capacity and increasing potential for transfer, ϕ′\phi
^{\prime} sacrifices transfer value. Formally:

 vϕ​(M)>vϕ′​(M)\displaystyle v_{\phi}({M})>v_{\phi^{\prime}}({M})
 ⇓\displaystyle\Downarrow
 ∃s,t∈𝒮:\displaystyle\exists s,t\in\mathcal{S}:
 1.ϕ⁡(s)≠ϕ⁡(t)2.ϕ′​(s)=ϕ′​(t)3.∃δ∈Πδ(ϕ,M):δ(⋅∣s)≠δ(⋅∣t)4.∀δ′∈Πδ(ϕ′,M):δ′(⋅∣s)=δ′(⋅∣t)\displaystyle
 \begin{aligned} &1.\;\phi(s)\neq\phi(t)\\ &2.\;\phi^{\prime}(s)=\phi^{\prime}(t)\\ &3.\;\exists\
 delta\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,{M}):\delta(\cdot\mid s)\neq\delta(\cdot\mid t)\\ &4.\;\forall\delta^{\
 prime}\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi^{\prime},{M}):\delta^{\prime}(\cdot\mid s)=\delta^{\prime}(\cdot\mid
 t)\end{aligned}

Note that ϕ\phi need not be finer than ϕ′\phi^{\prime}, so there can still exist two states s¯,t¯∈𝒮
\bar{s},\bar{t}\in\mathcal{S} where ϕ⁡(s¯)=ϕ⁡(t¯)\phi(\bar{s})=\phi(\bar{t}) and ϕ′​(s¯)≠ϕ′​(t¯)\
phi^{\prime}(\bar{s})\neq\phi^{\prime}(\bar{t}) (to contrast with the consequent above). This
result just means that there is at least one pair of states where the opposite is true.

For the proof see Appendix A on page Proof.

As a direct result of Theorem 2.3 we also have the following corollary. While it is weaker than the
theorem it does enjoy some brevity.

Corollary 2.4.

Let ϕ:𝒮↦Φ\phi:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\Phi and ϕ′:𝒮↦Φ′\phi^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\Phi^{\prime} be
two state abstractions such that ϕ\phi has strictly higher transfer value than ϕ′\phi^{\prime} for
some MDP M{M}. Then ϕ′\phi^{\prime} can both be no finer than ϕ\phi and no more control granular
than ϕ\phi. Formally:

 vϕ​(M)>vϕ′​(M)⟹ϕ′⋡ϕ∧Πδ​(ϕ,M)⊈Πδ​(ϕ′,M)v_{\phi}({M})>v_{\phi^{\prime}}({M})\Longrightarrow\phi
 ^{\prime}\not\succeq\phi\;\wedge\;\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,{M})\not\subseteq\Pi_{\delta}(\phi^{\      (8)
 prime},{M})

The following also holds. If ϕ\phi is coarser and less control granular than ϕ′\phi^{\prime}, it is
impossible for ϕ\phi to have a higher transfer value than ϕ′\phi^{\prime}. Formally:

 ϕ′⪰ϕ∧Πδ​(ϕ,M)⊆Πδ​(ϕ′,M)⟹vϕ​(M)≯vϕ′​(M)\phi^{\prime}\succeq\phi\;\wedge\;\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,
 {M})\subseteq\Pi_{\delta}(\phi^{\prime},{M})\Longrightarrow v_{\phi}({M})\not>v_{\phi^{\       (9)
 prime}}({M})

For the proof see Appendix A on page Proof.

We have established a general trade-off between transfer value on one hand and coarseness or
control granularity on the other hand. However, it’s not as simple as every coarsening resulting in
decreased transfer value and vice versa. It is sometimes possible to coarsen a state abstraction
without sacrificing transfer value. For example, the identity state abstraction has maximal
transfer value, yet there can be state abstractions with the same maximal transfer value yet that
are coarser that the identity state abstraction. This presents an interesting opportunity, where
potential for transfer can be increased with no drawback.

Definition 2.14 (minimal control state abstraction).

A state abstraction ϕ:𝒮↦Φ\phi:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\Phi is a minimal control state abstraction for an
MDP M=⟨𝒮,𝒜,p,r,γ⟩{M}=\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p,r,\gamma\rangle if ϕ\phi has maximal transfer
value and there exists no state abstraction coarser than ϕ\phi with higher transfer value than ϕ\
phi.

In other words, there is no coarsening a minimal control state abstraction without negatively
impacting transfer value because that would prevent the optimal policy from being derived. Still,
there is the potential for some increased coarseness compared to the identity state abstraction
where distinguishing between states does not impact the solution quality.

∗\ast ∗\ast ∗\ast

2.3.2 Multi-Task Transfer.

Now that we have considered transfer within a single task, a natural extension would be to consider
two tasks Mα=⟨𝒮α,𝒜,pα,rα⟩{M}_{\alpha}=\langle\mathcal{S}_{\alpha},\mathcal{A},p_{\alpha},r_{\alpha}
\rangle and Mβ=⟨𝒮β,𝒜,pβ,rβ⟩{M}_{\beta}=\langle\mathcal{S}_{\beta},\mathcal{A},p_{\beta},r_{\beta}\
rangle with a shared action set 𝒜\mathcal{A}. We can also consider two state abstractions ϕα:𝒮α↦Φα\
phi_{\alpha}:\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\mapsto\Phi_{\alpha} and ϕβ:𝒮β↦Φβ\phi_{\beta}:\mathcal{S}_{\beta}\
mapsto\Phi_{\beta} such that Φα∪Φβ⊆Φ\Phi_{\alpha}\cup\Phi_{\beta}\subseteq\Phi. While the images of
ϕα\phi_{\alpha} and ϕβ\phi_{\beta}, i.e. Φα\Phi_{\alpha} and Φβ\Phi_{\beta}, are not necessarily
the same, they might have a non-empty intersection. Both state abstractions define equivalence
relations on their respective domains, as discussed in the previous single-task setting. On top of
that we can also consider an equivalence relation relating states from the original sets 𝒮α\mathcal
{S}_{\alpha} and 𝒮β\mathcal{S}_{\beta} wherever they share an abstraction. We term this equivalence
relation Eϕα,ϕβE_{\phi_{\alpha},\phi_{\beta}} over 𝒮α∪𝒮β{\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}}\cup{\mathcal{S}_{\
beta}} the equivalence relation imposed by ϕα{\phi_{\alpha}} and ϕβ{\phi_{\beta}} and, if we take
ϕ⁡(s)\phi(s) to be ϕα​(s)\phi_{\alpha}(s) if s∈𝒮αs\in\mathcal{S}_{\alpha} and ϕβ​(s)\phi_{\beta}(s)
otherwise, define it for states s,s′∈𝒮α∪𝒮βs,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\cup\mathcal{S}_{\
beta} as:

 Eϕα,ϕβ​(s,s′)⇔ϕ⁡(s)=ϕ⁡(s′)E_{\phi_{\alpha},\phi_{\beta}}(s,s^{\prime})\iff\phi(s)=\phi(s^{\   (10)
 prime})

This is a generalization of the single-task setting in Eq. (5). If we now learn a policy πϕα:𝒮ϕα↦𝒫⁡
(𝒜)\pi_{\phi_{\alpha}}:\mathcal{S}_{\phi_{\alpha}}\mapsto~\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A}) for the abstract
MDP Mϕα{M}_{\phi_{\alpha}}, it can be readily transferred to the ground task Mα{M}_{\alpha} using
policy derivation. Moreover, if Φα\Phi_{\alpha} and Φβ\Phi_{\beta} intersect, there will be states
sα∈𝒮αs_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{S}_{\alpha} and sβ∈𝒮βs_{\beta}\in\mathcal{S}_{\beta} so that ϕ⁡(sα)=ϕ⁡
(sβ)\phi(s_{\alpha})=\phi(s_{\beta}). For these states sβs_{\beta}, it is possible to again
construct a policy by setting πβ(⋅|sβ)=πϕα(⋅|ϕβ(sβ))\pi_{\beta}(\cdot|s_{\beta})=\pi_{\phi_{\
alpha}}(\cdot|{\phi_{\beta}}(s_{\beta})). This may not cover the whole state set 𝒮β\mathcal{S}_{\
beta}—a policy πβ\pi_{\beta} so constructed would not necessarily constitute a full solution to Mβ
{M}_{\beta}—but it could still improve learning in Mβ{M}_{\beta} by bootstrapping with a partial
solution. In this setting we refer to Mβ{M}_{\beta} as the target task, and to Mα{M}_{\alpha} as
the source task. The part of the target task’s state set that is covered by the overlap in state
abstractions we refer to as the transfer cover.


Figure 3: Diagram detailing the multi-task transfer setting. Rectangles depict state sets while
squares denote states within those sets. The dark gray overlap denotes Φα∩Φβ{\Phi_{\alpha}}\cap{\
Phi_{\beta}} and the dotted arrows starting from it point at ground states in the transfer cover of
ϕβ{\phi_{\beta}} given ϕα{\phi_{\alpha}}.

Definition 2.15 (transfer cover).

Let ϕα:𝒮α↦Φα\phi_{\alpha}:\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\mapsto\Phi_{\alpha} and ϕβ:𝒮β↦Φβ\phi_{\beta}:\
mathcal{S}_{\beta}\mapsto\Phi_{\beta} be two state abstractions. We refer to the set of all ground
states sβ∈𝒮βs_{\beta}\in\mathcal{S}_{\beta} with an abstraction in the intersection of the two
abstractions Φα∩Φβ\Phi_{\alpha}\cap\Phi_{\beta} as the transfer cover of ϕβ\phi_{\beta} given ϕα\
phi_{\alpha} and denote it as

 ϕβ−1​(Φα)\displaystyle\phi^{-1} ≐{s∈ϕβ−1​(ϕ¯)|ϕ¯∈Φα}\displaystyle\mathrel{\doteq}\Set{s\in\phi^
 _{\beta}(\Phi_{\alpha})         {-1}_{\beta}(\bar{\phi})}{\bar{\phi}\in\Phi_{\alpha}}

This also implies a partial ordering over pairs of state abstractions. Let ϕβ′:𝒮β↦Φβ′\phi_{\beta}^
{\prime}:\mathcal{S}_{\beta}\mapsto\Phi_{\beta}^{\prime} be another state abstraction. Given ϕα\
phi_{\alpha}, ϕβ\phi_{\beta} has greater transfer cover than ϕβ′\phi_{\beta}^{\prime} if the
transfer cover of ϕβ′\phi_{\beta}^{\prime} is a subset of that of ϕβ\phi_{\beta}. That is, if:

 ϕβ′−1​(Φα)⊆ϕβ−1​(Φα){\phi_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{-1}(\Phi_{\alpha})\subseteq{\phi_{\beta}}^{-1}(\Phi_
 {\alpha})

Now that we have identified the overlap between state abstractions as an opportunity for transfer,
we can revisit policy derivation for the multi-task setting.

Definition 2.16 (partially derived policy).

Let Mα=⟨𝒮α,𝒜,pα,rα⟩{M}_{\alpha}=\langle\mathcal{S}_{\alpha},\mathcal{A},p_{\alpha},r_{\alpha}\
rangle and Mβ=⟨𝒮β,𝒜,pβ,rβ⟩{M}_{\beta}=\langle\mathcal{S}_{\beta},\mathcal{A},p_{\beta},r_{\beta}\
rangle be two MDPs, let ϕα:𝒮α↦Φα\phi_{\alpha}:\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\mapsto\Phi_{\alpha} and ϕβ:𝒮β↦Φβ
\phi_{\beta}:\mathcal{S}_{\beta}\mapsto\Phi_{\beta} be two state abstractions such that Φα∪Φβ⊆\Phi_
{\alpha}\cup\Phi_{\beta}\subseteq, and let πϕα:Φα↦𝒫⁡(𝒜)\pi_{\phi_{\alpha}}:\Phi_{\alpha}\mapsto\
mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A}) be an abstract policy. A policy δ​a​β:𝒮β↦𝒫⁡(𝒜)\delta a\beta:\mathcal{S}_{\
beta}\mapsto\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A}) is partially derived if it holds that ∀sβ∈ϕβ−1​(Φα):\forall s_
{\beta}\in\phi_{\beta}^{-1}(\Phi_{\alpha}):

 δ​a​β​(a|sβ)=πϕα​(a|ϕβ​(sβ))\delta a\beta(a|s_{\beta})=\pi_{\phi_{\alpha}}(a|{\phi_{\beta}}(s_{\
 beta}))

Let Πϕα\Pi_{\phi_{\alpha}} be the set of all abstract policies πϕα:Φα↦𝒫⁡(𝒜)\pi_{\phi_{\alpha}}:\
Phi_{\alpha}\mapsto\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A}). We write the set of partially derived policies from
state abstraction ϕα\phi_{\alpha} for state abstraction ϕβ\phi_{\beta} and MDP Mβ{M}_{\beta} as

 Πδ(ϕβ,Mβ;ϕα)≐{δ​a​β∈Πδ​(ϕβ,M)|∃πϕα∈Πϕα:∀sβ∈ϕ−1β(Φα):δaβ(⋅|sβ)=πϕα(⋅|ϕβ(sβ))}\Pi_{\delta}(\phi_{\
 beta},{M}_{\beta};\phi_{\alpha})\mathrel{\doteq}\Set{\delta a\beta\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi_{\
 beta},M)}{\exists\pi_{\phi_{\alpha}}\in\Pi_{\phi_{\alpha}}:\forall s_{\beta}\in\phi^{-1}_{\beta}
 (\Phi_{\alpha}):\delta a\beta(\cdot|s_{\beta})=\pi_{\phi_{\alpha}}(\cdot|\phi_{\beta}(s_{\
 beta}))}

We can also consider the subset of partially derived policies for a single abstract policy. We
write the set of partially derived policies from abstract policy πϕα\pi_{\phi_{\alpha}} for state
abstraction ϕβ{\phi_{\beta}} and MDP Mβ{{M}_{\beta}} as

 Πδ(ϕβ,Mβ;πϕα)≐{δ​a​β∈Πδ​(ϕβ,M)|∀sβ∈ϕ−1β(Φα):δaβ(⋅|sβ)=πϕα(⋅|ϕβ(sβ))}\Pi_{\delta}(\phi_{\beta},{M}
 _{\beta};\pi_{\phi_{\alpha}})\mathrel{\doteq}\Set{\delta a\beta\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi_{\beta},M)}{\
 forall s_{\beta}\in\phi^{-1}_{\beta}(\Phi_{\alpha}):\delta a\beta(\cdot|s_{\beta})=\pi_{\phi_{\
 alpha}}(\cdot|\phi_{\beta}(s_{\beta}))}

It is straightforward to see that single-task derived policies form a special case of cross-task
partially derived policies by setting Mα=Mβ{M}_{\alpha}={M}_{\beta} and ϕα=ϕβ\phi_{\alpha}=\phi_{\
beta}. The single-task setting is in effect a special case of the multi-task setting with full
overlap between the abstract codomains resulting in the partially derived policy making up a
complete policy.

Whenever Φβ⊈Φα{\Phi_{\beta}}\not\subseteq{\Phi_{\alpha}}, that is, when ϕβ\phi_{\beta} does not
have maximal transfer cover given ϕα\phi_{\alpha}, the above partially derived policy construction
does not constitute a full policy for all states in Φβ\Phi_{\beta}, hence the partial in the name.
Since the region of the state space described by the transfer cover is the only place where
transfer can occur, we should want it to be as great as possible. At the same time, there exists an
interesting interplay between transfer cover and concepts we have discussed before in the
single-task setting: control granularity and transfer value. We will now re-visit these concepts
for the multi-task setting, taking into account their interactions with transfer cover.

Definition 2.17 (conditional coarseness).

Let ϕα:𝒮α↦Φα\phi_{\alpha}:\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\mapsto\Phi_{\alpha}, ϕβ:𝒮β↦Φβ\phi_{\beta}:\mathcal
{S}_{\beta}\mapsto\Phi_{\beta}, ϕβ′:𝒮β↦Φβ′\phi_{\beta}^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}_{\beta}\mapsto\Phi^{\
prime}_{\beta} be state abstractions such that Φα∪Φβ∪Φβ′\Phi_{\alpha}\cup\Phi_{\beta}\cup\Phi^{\
prime}_{\beta}. We say that ϕβ\phi_{\beta} is finer given ϕα\phi_{\alpha} than ϕβ′\phi_{\beta}^{\
prime}, denoted ϕβ​⪰ϕα​ϕβ′\phi_{\beta}\underset{\phi_{\alpha}}{\succeq}\phi_{\beta}^{\prime}, if
all states in the transfer cover of ϕβ\phi_{\beta} given ϕα\phi_{\alpha} that are equivalent under
ϕβ\phi_{\beta} are also in the transfer cover of ϕβ′\phi_{\beta}^{\prime} given ϕα\phi_{\alpha} and
are also equivalent under ϕβ′\phi_{\beta}^{\prime}. Formally:

 ϕβ​⪰ϕα​ϕβ′⇕∀s,t∈𝒮β:ϕβ​(s)=ϕβ​(t)∧s,t∈ϕβ−1​(Φα)⟹ϕβ′​(s)=ϕβ′​(t)∧s,t∈ϕβ′−1​(Φα)\begin{gathered}\
 phi_{\beta}\underset{\phi_{\alpha}}{\succeq}\phi_{\beta}^{\prime}\\ \Updownarrow\\ \forall s,t\in
 \mathcal{S}_{\beta}:\phi_{\beta}(s)=\phi_{\beta}(t)\wedge s,t\in\phi_{\beta}^{-1}(\Phi_{\alpha})
 \;\Longrightarrow\;\phi_{\beta}^{\prime}(s)=\phi_{\beta}^{\prime}(t)\wedge s,t\in{\phi_{\beta}^{\
 prime}}^{-1}(\Phi_{\alpha})\end{gathered}

If, on the other hand, it holds that ϕβ​⪯ϕα​ϕβ′\phi_{\beta}\underset{\phi_{\alpha}}{\preceq}\phi_{\
beta}^{\prime} (shorthand for ϕβ′​⪰ϕα​ϕβ\phi^{\prime}_{\beta}\underset{\phi_{\alpha}}{\succeq}\phi_
{\beta}), we say that ϕβ\phi_{\beta} is coarser given ϕα\phi_{\alpha} than ϕβ′\phi_{\beta}^{\
prime}. If, in addition, ϕβ\phi_{\beta} is not isomorphic to ϕβ′\phi_{\beta}^{\prime}, we say that
ϕβ\phi_{\beta} is strictly finer (resp. strictly coarser) than ϕβ′\phi_{\beta}^{\prime}.

The difference with the single state abstraction version we used before is that this definition of
coarseness takes into account the transfer cover. It is possible for one state abstraction to be
strictly finer than another without being strictly finer with respect to a given state abstraction
ϕα\phi_{\alpha}, simply because only states in the transfer cover are relevant for conditional
coarseness. In effect, in our transfer setting, part of the abstract state set is left outside of
consideration. Specifically, the part of Φβ\Phi_{\beta} that does not overlap with Φα\Phi_{\alpha},
i.e. Φβ−Φα\Phi_{\beta}-\Phi_{\alpha}.


(a)


(b)


Figure 4: Two transfer settings, each showing the same two states with two different state
abstractions. In each setting the abstract state ψ¯2\bar{\psi}_{2} falls outside of the transfer
cover.

Since transfer cover requires exact equality between abstract states in Φα\Phi_{\alpha} and Φβ\Phi_
{\beta} and coarseness includes equality under isomorphism, the interplay between transfer cover
and coarseness can be non-obvious. It is for example possible for a state abstraction to have
different transfer cover but the same level of conditional coarseness, by means of a simple
relabelling of abstract states. This relabelling is a bijection, i.e. an isomorphism, and preserves
conditional coarseness. Figure 4 shows two transfer settings where shaded abstract states depict
states in the transfer cover. In each setting, ϕ\phi is equally fine as ϕ′\phi^{\prime}. However,
in the first setting (Figure 4(a)) ϕ\phi has less transfer cover than ϕ′\phi^{\prime}, and in the
second setting (Figure 4(b)) ϕ\phi has more transfer cover. The difference in each case is that one
abstract state is simply relabelled as ψ¯2\bar{\psi}_{2}, i.e. a state that is not in the transfer
cover. While conditional coarseness is preserved, transfer cover can be affected as transfer cover
requires exact equality. From this perspective transfer cover is a more stringent property, one
that is necessary for transfer. This also means that there is no straightforward relation between a
change in coarseness and a change in transfer cover. Just like in the single-task setting however,
there does exist a direct relation between conditional coarseness and and conditional control
granularity.

Definition 2.18 (conditional control granularity).

Let ϕα:𝒮α↦Φα\phi_{\alpha}:\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\mapsto\Phi_{\alpha}, ϕβ:𝒮β↦Φβ\phi_{\beta}:\mathcal
{S}_{\beta}\mapsto\Phi_{\beta}, and ϕβ′:𝒮β↦Φβ′\phi_{\beta}^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}_{\beta}\mapsto\Phi_
{\beta}^{\prime} be state abstractions such that Φα∪Φβ∪Φβ′\Phi_{\alpha}\cup\Phi_{\beta}\cup\Phi^{\
prime}_{\beta}, and let Mβ=⟨𝒮β,𝒜,pβ,rβ⟩{M}_{\beta}=\langle\mathcal{S}_{\beta},\mathcal{A},p_{\
beta},r_{\beta}\rangle be an MDP. We say that ϕβ\phi_{\beta} is more control granular for Mβ{M}_{\
beta} given ϕα\phi_{\alpha} than ϕβ′\phi_{\beta}^{\prime} if all derived policies contained in Πδ​
(ϕβ′,Mβ,ϕα)\Pi_{\delta}(\phi_{\beta}^{\prime},{M}_{\beta};\phi_{\alpha}) are also contained in Πδ​
(ϕβ′,Mβ,ϕα)\Pi_{\delta}(\phi_{\beta}^{\prime},{M}_{\beta};\phi_{\alpha}).

 Πδ​(ϕβ′,M,ϕα)⊆Πδ​(ϕβ,M,ϕα)⟺∀δ′∈Πδ​(ϕβ′,M,ϕα):∃δ∈Πδ​(ϕβ,M,ϕα):δ=δ′\Pi_{\delta}(\phi_{\beta}^{\
 prime},{M};\phi_{\alpha})\subseteq\Pi_{\delta}(\phi_{\beta},{M};\phi_{\alpha})\;\
 Longleftrightarrow\;\forall\delta^{\prime}\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi_{\beta}^{\prime},{M};\phi_{\
 alpha}):\exists\delta\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi_{\beta},{M};\phi_{\alpha}):\delta=\delta^{\prime}

If in addition Πδ​(ϕβ′,M,ϕα)≠Πδ​(ϕβ,M,ϕα)\Pi_{\delta}(\phi_{\beta}^{\prime},{M};\phi_{\alpha})\neq\
Pi_{\delta}(\phi_{\beta},{M};\phi_{\alpha}), we say that ϕβ\phi_{\beta} is strictly more control
granular than ϕβ′\phi_{\beta}^{\prime} given ϕα\phi_{\alpha}.

Proposition 2.5 (control granularity–coarseness trade-off).

Let ϕα:𝒮α↦Φα\phi_{\alpha}:\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\mapsto\Phi_{\alpha}, ϕβ:𝒮β↦Φβ\phi_{\beta}:\mathcal
{S}_{\beta}\mapsto\Phi_{\beta}, and ϕβ′:𝒮β↦Φβ′\phi_{\beta}^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}_{\beta}\mapsto\Phi^
{\prime}_{\beta} be state abstractions such that Φα∪Φβ∪Φβ′\Phi_{\alpha}\cup\Phi_{\beta}\cup\Phi^{\
prime}_{\beta} and let Mβ=⟨𝒮β,𝒜,pβ,rβ⟩{M}_{\beta}=\langle\mathcal{S}_{\beta},\mathcal{A},p_{\
beta},r_{\beta}\rangle be an MDP. Then ϕβ\phi_{\beta} is finer given ϕα\phi_{\alpha} than ϕβ′\phi_
{\beta}^{\prime} if and only if ϕβ\phi_{\beta} is more control granular given Mβ{M}_{\beta} and ϕα\
phi_{\alpha} than ϕβ′\phi_{\beta}^{\prime}. Formally:

 ϕβ​⪰ϕα​ϕβ′⟺Πδ​(ϕβ,Mβ,ϕα)⊇Πδ​(ϕβ′,Mβ,ϕα)\phi_{\beta}\underset{\phi_{\alpha}}{\succeq}\phi_{\beta}^
 {\prime}\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\Pi_{\delta}(\phi_{\beta},{M}_{\beta};\phi_{\alpha})\supseteq\Pi_
 {\delta}(\phi_{\beta}^{\prime},{M}_{\beta};\phi_{\alpha})

The proof is a straightforward extension of the proof of the single-task state abstraction
Proposition 2.2. We omit it here for the sake of brevity.

Just like in the single-task setting, state abstractions can impact attainable value functions in
the multi-task setting. We can compare attainable value functions between two state abstractions
for an MDP, conditional on another state abstraction (based on transfer cover).

Definition 2.19 (conditional transfer value).

Let ϕα:𝒮α↦Φα\phi_{\alpha}:\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\mapsto\Phi_{\alpha}, ϕβ:𝒮β↦Φβ\phi_{\beta}:\mathcal
{S}_{\beta}\mapsto\Phi_{\beta}, ϕβ′:𝒮β↦Φβ′\phi_{\beta}^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}_{\beta}\mapsto\Phi^{\
prime}_{\beta} be state abstractions such that Φα∪Φβ∪Φβ′\Phi_{\alpha}\cup\Phi_{\beta}\cup\Phi^{\
prime}_{\beta} and let Mβ=⟨𝒮β,𝒜,pβ,rβ,γ⟩{M}_{\beta}=\langle\mathcal{S}_{\beta},\mathcal{A},p_{\
beta},r_{\beta},\gamma\rangle be an MDP. We say that ϕβ\phi_{\beta} has greater transfer value for
Mβ{M}_{\beta} given ϕα\phi_{\alpha} than ϕβ′\phi_{\beta^{\prime}}, denoted vϕβ​(Mβ,ϕα)>vϕβ′​(Mβ,ϕα)
v_{\phi_{\beta}}({M}_{\beta};\phi_{\alpha})>v_{\phi_{\beta}^{\prime}}({M}_{\beta};\phi_{\alpha}),
if there exists a derived policy for ϕβ\phi_{\beta} with a value function greater than or equal to
all derived policies’ value functions for ϕβ′\phi_{\beta}^{\prime}:

 vϕβ​(Mβ,ϕα)≥vϕβ′​(Mβ,ϕα)⇕∃δβ∈Πδ​(ϕβ,Mβ,ϕα):∀δβ′∈Πδ​(ϕβ′,Mβ,ϕα):vδβ​(⋅,Mβ)≥vδβ′​(⋅,Mβ)\begin
 {gathered}v_{\phi_{\beta}}({M}_{\beta};\phi_{\alpha})\geq v_{\phi_{\beta}^{\prime}}({M}_{\beta};\
 phi_{\alpha})\\ \Updownarrow\\ \exists\delta_{\beta}\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi_{\beta},{M}_{\beta};\
 phi_{\alpha}):\forall\delta_{\beta}^{\prime}\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi_{\beta}^{\prime},{M}_{\beta};\
 phi_{\alpha}):v_{\delta_{\beta}}(\cdot;{M}_{\beta})\geq v_{\delta_{\beta}^{\prime}}(\cdot;{M}_{\
 beta})\end{gathered}

If the above inequality is strict, we say that ϕβ\phi_{\beta} has strictly greater transfer value
than ϕβ′\phi_{\beta}^{\prime}.

As is the case in the single-task setting, so too in the multi-task setting are control granularity
and coarseness on one side and transfer value on the other side related. This time it takes into
account the transfer cover.

Theorem 2.6 (transfer value–control trade-off).

Let ϕα:𝒮α↦Φα\phi_{\alpha}:\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\mapsto\Phi_{\alpha}, ϕβ:𝒮β↦Φβ\phi_{\beta}:\mathcal
{S}_{\beta}\mapsto\Phi_{\beta}, and ϕβ′:𝒮β↦Φβ′\phi_{\beta}^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}_{\beta}\mapsto\Phi^
{\prime}_{\beta} be state abstractions such that Φα∪Φβ∪Φβ′\Phi_{\alpha}\cup\Phi_{\beta}\cup\Phi^{\
prime}_{\beta} and such that ϕβ\phi_{\beta} has strictly higher transfer value given ϕα\phi_{\
alpha} for an MDP Mβ=⟨𝒮β,𝒜,pβ,rβ⟩{M}_{\beta}=\langle\mathcal{S}_{\beta},\mathcal{A},p_{\beta},r_{\
beta}\rangle than ϕβ′\phi_{\beta}^{\prime}. Then there must exist two states in the transfer cover
of ϕβ′\phi_{\beta}^{\prime} given ϕα\phi_{\alpha} such that ϕβ′\phi_{\beta}^{\prime} does not
distinguish between them while either ϕβ\phi_{\beta} does, or not both states are in the transfer
cover of ϕβ\phi_{\beta}. In addition, there exists a partially derived policy for ϕβ\phi_{\beta}
that takes advantage of this by controlling ss and tt differently in a way that no partially
derived policy of ϕβ′\phi_{\beta}^{\prime} can do. Formally:

 vϕβ​(Mβ,ϕα)>vϕβ′​(Mβ,ϕα)\displaystyle v_{\phi_{\beta}}({M}_{\beta};\phi_{\alpha})>v_{\phi_{\beta}
 ^{\prime}}({M}_{\beta};\phi_{\alpha})
 ⇓\displaystyle\Downarrow
 ∃s,t∈𝒮β:\displaystyle\exists s,t\in\mathcal{S}_{\beta}:
 1.ϕβ​(s)≠ϕβ​(t)∨s,t∉ϕβ−1​(Φα)2.ϕβ′​(s)=ϕβ′​(t)∧s,t∈ϕβ′−1​(Φα)3.∃δβ∈Πδ(ϕβ,Mβ;ϕα):δβ(⋅∣s)≠δβ(⋅∣t)
 4.∀δβ′∈Πδ(ϕβ′,Mβ;ϕα):δβ′(⋅∣s)=δβ′(⋅∣t)\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &1.\;\phi_{\beta}(s)\neq\phi_
 {\beta}(t)\vee s,t\not\in{\phi_{\beta}}^{-1}(\Phi_{\alpha})\\ &2.\;\phi_{\beta}^{\prime}(s)=\phi_
 {\beta}^{\prime}(t)\wedge s,t\in{\phi_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{-1}(\Phi_{\alpha})\\ &3.\;\exists\delta_
 {\beta}\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi_{\beta},{M}_{\beta};\phi_{\alpha}):\delta_{\beta}(\cdot\mid s)\neq\
 delta_{\beta}(\cdot\mid t)\\ &4.\;\forall\delta_{\beta^{\prime}}\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi_{\beta}^{\
 prime},{M}_{\beta};\phi_{\alpha}):\delta_{\beta^{\prime}}(\cdot\mid s)=\delta_{\beta^{\prime}}(\
 cdot\mid t)\end{aligned}

The proof of this theorem proceeds exactly like that of Theorem 2.3 with the added limitation
imposed by transfer cover. Analogous to the single task setting we derive the following corollary.

Corollary 2.7.

Let ϕα:𝒮α↦Φα\phi_{\alpha}:\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\mapsto\Phi_{\alpha}, ϕβ:𝒮β↦Φβ\phi_{\beta}:\mathcal
{S}_{\beta}\mapsto\Phi_{\beta}, and ϕβ′:𝒮β↦Φβ\phi_{\beta}^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}_{\beta}\mapsto\Phi_
{\beta} be state abstractions such that Φα∪Φβ\Phi_{\alpha}\cup\Phi_{\beta} and such that ϕβ\phi_{\
beta} has strictly higher transfer value given ϕα\phi_{\alpha} for an MDP Mβ=⟨𝒮β,𝒜,pβ,rβ⟩{M}_{\
beta}=\langle\mathcal{S}_{\beta},\mathcal{A},p_{\beta},r_{\beta}\rangle than ϕβ′\phi_{\beta}^{\
prime}. Then ϕβ′\phi_{\beta}^{\prime} can both be no finer given ϕα\phi_{\alpha} than ϕβ\phi_{\
beta} and no more control granular given ϕα\phi_{\alpha} than ϕβ\phi_{\beta}. Formally:

 vϕβ​(Mβ,ϕα)>vϕβ′​(Mβ,ϕα)⟹ϕβ′​⪰ϕα​ϕβ∧Πδ​(ϕβ,Mβ,ϕα)⊈Πδ​(ϕβ′,Mβ,ϕα)v_{\phi_{\beta}}({M}_{\
 beta};\phi_{\alpha})>v_{\phi_{\beta}^{\prime}}({M}_{\beta};\phi_{\alpha})\Longrightarrow\
 phi_{\beta}^{\prime}\not\underset{\phi_{\alpha}}{\succeq}\phi_{\beta}\;\wedge\;\Pi_{\delta}   (11)
 (\phi_{\beta},{M}_{\beta};\phi_{\alpha})\not\subseteq\Pi_{\delta}(\phi_{\beta}^{\prime},{M}_
 {\beta};\phi_{\alpha})

The following also holds. If ϕβ\phi_{\beta} is coarser and less control granular given ϕα\phi_{\
alpha} than ϕβ′\phi_{\beta}^{\prime}, it is impossible for ϕβ\phi_{\beta} to have a higher transfer
value given ϕα\phi_{\alpha} than ϕβ′\phi_{\beta}^{\prime}. Formally:

 ϕβ′​⪰ϕα​ϕβ∧Πδ​(ϕβ,Mβ,ϕα)⊆Πδ​(ϕβ′,Mβ,ϕα)⟹vϕβ​(Mβ,ϕα)≯vϕβ′​(Mβ,ϕα)\phi_{\beta}^{\prime}\
 underset{\phi_{\alpha}}{\succeq}\phi_{\beta}\;\wedge\;\Pi_{\delta}(\phi_{\beta},{M}_{\beta};
 \phi_{\alpha})\subseteq\Pi_{\delta}(\phi_{\beta}^{\prime},{M}_{\beta};\phi_{\alpha})\         (12)
 Longrightarrow v_{\phi_{\beta}}({M}_{\beta};\phi_{\alpha})\not>v_{\phi_{\beta}^{\prime}}({M}
 _{\beta};\phi_{\alpha})

Ideally we’d have maximal transfer cover and maximal transfer value. Maximal transfer cover means
that a partially derived policy is complete: behavior is transferred for every single target state.
Nothing more needs to be learned for the target task. Maximum transfer value in addition means that
it is possible for a derived policy to be optimal everywhere. This is no guarantee that it will be
but it does guarantee that the optimal policy is not excluded by means of policy derivation. Having
both maximal transfer cover and transfer value is an ideal situation, though impossible in all but
the most trivial cases. For some intuition behind this, consider when transfer cover is highest. If
one task is a subtask of another then behavior might be transferred readily and beneficially to the
subtask. In less trivial settings where this is not the case, there simply are no source states
from which we could substitute the behavior for every single target state. It is always possible to
arbitrarily increase transfer cover by increasing overlap between the abstract state sets Φα\Phi_{\
alpha} and Φβ\Phi_{\beta}, arbitrarily equating states across tasks. However, this overlap is not
necessarily beneficial, as would be reflected in the transfer value. In general, there is no free
lunch; there is no way for a given transfer setting to maximise both transfer cover and transfer
value. That summarised, we end this section with a definition of those state abstractions that do
in fact not sacrifice on transfer value while still being as coarse as possible.

Definition 2.20 (minimal control state abstraction).

A state abstraction ϕβ:𝒮β↦Φβ\phi_{\beta}:\mathcal{S}_{\beta}\mapsto\Phi_{\beta} in a minimal
control state abstraction for an MDP Mβ=⟨𝒮β,𝒜,pβ,rβ⟩{M}_{\beta}=\langle\mathcal{S}_{\beta},\mathcal
{A},p_{\beta},r_{\beta}\rangle given a state abstraction ϕα:𝒮α↦Φα\phi_{\alpha}:\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
\mapsto\Phi_{\alpha} if ϕβ\phi_{\beta} has maximal transfer value given ϕα\phi_{\alpha} and there
exists no state abstraction coarser given ϕα\phi_{\alpha} than ϕβ\phi_{\beta} that has higher
transfer value for Mβ{{M}_{\beta}} than ϕβ\phi_{\beta}.

As in the single task setting, a minimal control state abstraction cannot be coarsened without
negatively impacting transfer value. Since multi-task coarsening can result from increasing
transfer cover, this means that a minimal control state abstraction can also not increase its
transfer cover without affecting transfer value.

We have described here a framework to assess state abstractions and the opportunities for transfer
that they provide. What we have not gone into detail about is how to actually arrive at such state
abstractions. It is this topic that forms the heart of Section 3 and this paper as a whole. Before
we get there however, we will briefly introduce the basic setting of hierarchical reinforcement
learning, which will be the framework in which we will leverage our newly developed and assessed
state abstractions.

2.4 Skills as Options and Hierarchical Reinforcement Learning

Now that we have discussed state abstraction at length, it is time we discuss its counterpart:
action abstraction. A complete discussion building on the state abstractions we develop in the rest
of this paper can be found in Section 4. This section will be limited to laying the foundations.

In this paper we rely on the well-known formalism of options (Sutton et al., 1999), useful for its
versatility and lack of assumptions. Options can be seen as macro-actions that encapsulate a
particular behavior. In particular, we focus on options that describe local and reusable behavior.
To capture these notions we refer to such options as skills, as skills in common parlance describe
a very specific (and repeatedly applicable) behavior.

To formalize our notion of skills, we extend the traditional definition of options with a state set
that the option operates on.

Definition 2.21 (option).

An option ω\omega is a tuple ⟨𝒮ω,πω,βω⟩\langle\mathcal{S}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega},\beta_{\omega}\
rangle, with 𝒮ω\mathcal{S}_{\omega} the option’s domain, and

  πω:𝒮ω↦𝒫⁡(𝒜)\displaystyle\pi_{\omega}:\mathcal{S}_{\omega}\mapsto\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})
  βω:𝒮ω↦𝒫⁡({0,1})\displaystyle\beta_{\omega}:\mathcal{S}_{\omega}\mapsto\mathcal{P}(\{0,1\})

the option control policy and termination policy respectively.

Given an MDP M{M} with action set 𝒜\mathcal{A} and an option set Ω={ω∣ω=⟨𝒮ω,πω,βω⟩}\Omega=\mathopen
{}\mathclose{{\left\{\omega\mid\omega=\langle\mathcal{S}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega},\beta_{\omega}\
rangle}}\right\}, we construct an option-enabled MDP (also known as a Semi-MDP; (Sutton et al.,
1999)) MΩ{M}_{\Omega} with action set 𝒜∪Ω\mathcal{A}\cup\Omega. Intuitively, the agent expands its
action set with the set of options and can use them interchangeably, making no distinction between
primitive action (a∈𝒜a\in\mathcal{A}) or option. Upon invocation, an option’s control policy
selects primitive actions until a 11 is sampled from its termination policy indicating termination.
Upon termination, control is passed back to the agent, for whom only a single step has passed.

There is a form of action abstraction at play here. While multiple primitive steps may have been
taken by the option, to the agent it appears as only one action. This action abstraction leads to a
hierarchy, with the option-enabled agent initiating options on the higher level and the option
operating on the lower level. This view of options inducing a hierarchy has led to the term
hierarchical reinforcement learning (HRL) (Barto and Mahadevan, 2003).

A skill is defined for a particular state set – its domain. When its domain is chosen to be
task-independent the skill itself becomes usable across tasks. The hard part is of course to find
such a task-independent domain. Skills go hand in hand with state abstraction, the latter providing
the abstract state set for a skill to operate over. We will return to combining action abstraction
and state abstraction in Section 4 when we define options over our state abstractions that we
introduce in Section 3.2.

3 State Abstractions for Transfer

Whereas Section 2 dealt mainly with state abstractions and different ways to describe and compare
them, this section deals mainly with the construction of useful state abstractions. ‘Useful’, as
discussed in the previous section, generally means high transfer cover and high transfer value, but
that too will receive more nuance in this section as we explore how a state abstraction
construction impacts transfer value. In particular, in this section we propose a new method for
state abstraction. Based on predictions of outcomes, i.e. common characteristics between tasks,
(Section 3.1) outcome equivalent state abstractions come with strong guarantees of optimality when
used with a particular class of MDPs (Section 3.2). We finish with an illustration of what our
outcome-based state abstraction affords us in Section 3.4, showing that outcome equivalent state
abstractions can be coarsened to increase opportunity for transfer while incurring a cost in
optimality. This neatly leads into Section 4 which illustrates that this cost can be overcome,
meaning that you can have your cake and eat it too.

3.1 Outcomes as Reward Signals

So far we have not formally defined the notion of a domain of related tasks. We want to be able to
describe tasks that on the surface have something in common, where transitions across tasks follow
the same rules. In order to do so we first need a way to describe transitions. We start off with
remarkable transitions: transitions that are especially meaningful to the task at hand. It will
then be possible to describe transitions that are remarkable in the same way across tasks — a step
closer to describing related tasks.

We first introduce the notion of an outcome. Our notion of outcomes is inspired by the homonymous
outcomes of Sherstov and Stone (2005) that describe differences in state, tracing back to outcomes
in planning (Boutilier et al., 2001), and more recently by the reward decomposition of Barreto et
al. (2017).

A transition can be described in terms of multiple outcomes, each denoting the occurrence of
something remarkable. In particular, remarkable here is taken to mean ‘relevant to reward’: the
occurrence of an outcome signals the presence of reward. Or, from a different perspective, a
transition with no outcomes is a transition with no reward.

Just like rewards can be randomly distributed for a given transition, so are outcomes. Where we use
the random variable Rt+1R_{t+1} to describe reward for a transition, we use Σt+1\Sigma_{t+1} to do
the same for the outcome distribution. The difference is that while Rt+1R_{t+1} ranges over real
numbers, Σt+1\Sigma_{t+1} describes a distribution over vectors of outcomes. The motivation for
this is that multiple things may be relevant for reward, each in a different way. We rarely use
Rt+1R_{t+1} directly and usually use the expected reward for a transition (s,a,s′)(s,a,s^{\prime})
instead, denoted r⁡(s,a,s′)r(s,a,s^{\prime}). In the same vein we use σ⁡(s,a,s′)\sigma(s,a,s^{\
prime}) to denote the expected outcome for that transition. In addition, for simplicity we assume
that the expected outcome is itself an outcome. We will discuss later how this outcome function can
be obtained.

Definition 3.1 (outcome).

The random variable describing outcomes at time t+1{t+1} is denoted Σt+1\Sigma_{t+1} and is defined
over the outcome domain Σ\Sigma. Given a transition (s,a,s′)∈𝒮×𝒜×𝒮(s,a,s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\
times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}, its expected outcomes are given by σ⁡(s,a,s′)∈Σ\sigma(s,a,s^{\
prime})~\in~\Sigma, with σ⁡(s,a,s′)\sigma(s,a,s^{\prime}) defined as 𝐸[Σt+1∣St=s,At=a,St+1=s′]\
mathop{\mathbb{E}}[\Sigma_{t+1}\mid S_{t}=s,A_{t}=a,S_{t+1}=s^{\prime}]. The outcome domain Σ\Sigma
is defined as Σ⊆Rd\Sigma\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}. Finally, the expected reward of a transition
(s,a,s′)∈𝒮×𝒜×𝒮(s,a,s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S} can be decomposed as
a product of the expected outcomes of that transition and a task-specific reward weights vector
wr∈Rdw_{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{d} which describes how different outcomes affect the reward for that task:

 r⁡(s,a,s′)=σ​(s,a,s′)⊤​wr,r(s,a,s^{\prime})=\sigma(s,a,s^{\prime})^{\top}w_{r},  (13)

If this holds for all transitions we can also write r=σ⊤​wrr=\sigma^{\top}w_{r} which we define to
only hold when the above equality holds for all transitions in the domain 𝒮×𝒜×𝒮\mathcal{S}\times\
mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}. Since we do not work with MDPs that have randomly distributed reward
given an exact transition (s,a,s′)(s,a,s^{\prime}), we instead refer to expected outcomes as
outcomes.

An outcome’s presence is indicated by a non-zero element in σ⁡(s,a,s′)\sigma(s,a,s^{\prime}).
Similarly, a zero element means the outcome did not occur. If all elements are 00 it means the
transition is not remarkable in any way, i.e. it is not relevant for reward.

Example 3.1.



Figure 5: Two gridworld tasks with different layouts that belong to the same domain.

For example, Figure 5 describes two Gridworlds. In both tasks transitioning onto a green tile
yields a positive reward of 1010 and terminates the episode. Transitioning onto a red tile incurs a
negative reward depending on the task at hand: −10-10 in the first task and −1-1 in the second
task. Both tasks seem to be composed of the same elements; red and green tiles are present in both
tasks and both incur rewards, albeit that the specific amount differs. These elements are part of
the language of the domain that both tasks belong to and outcomes are our tool to describe them.

To continue the example, we can describe the outcomes for both tasks as a binary-valued vector

 σ⁡(s,a,s′)=(green​(s′),red​(s′))⊤,\sigma(s,a,s^{\prime})=(\texttt{green}(s^{\prime}),\texttt{red}
 (s^{\prime}))^{\top},

where green​(s)\texttt{green}(s) and red​(s)\texttt{red}(s) take on a value of 11 only if the agent
in state ss occupies a tile of the corresponding colour. That is, there are two possible outcomes
and they describe where the agent ends up after a transition. In this example, we have d=2d=2
because there are two outcomes corresponding to the two different colors. The reward weights vector
for the first task is wrα=(10,−10)⊤w_{r_{\alpha}}=(10,-10)^{\top} and for the second task we have
wrβ=(10,−1)⊤w_{r_{\beta}}=(10,-1)^{\top}. The difference between the reward weights for both tasks
highlights the aforementioned difference in rewards; the second task penalizes transitioning onto
red cells less heavily.

Transitions with rewards are not the only remarkable transitions in this example. Both tasks
contain impassable walls and attempted movement in the direction of a wall will fail in both tasks.
This, just like transitioning onto red or green cells, is also a kind of transition that exists in
both tasks and is part of the elements that make up the domain. It may therefore be useful to
expand the outcome function σ\sigma to include other outcomes that are not relevant to reward. This
irrelevance will show itself in corresponding elements of wrw_{r} which are set to 00. As a result,
the outcome function σ\sigma can always be expanded while maintaining correctness. We will revisit
this idea of non-necessary outcomes later. ∎

To summarize the above, outcomes allow describing reward functions of different tasks in terms of
invariant commonalities. These commonalities make up the language of what we have so far informally
referred to as domains. We formalize a domain for the purposes of our work as follows.

Definition 3.2 (domain).

A domain 𝒟\mathcal{D} is a set of tasks with the same outcome domain Σ\Sigma:

 𝒟≐{M|M=⟨𝒮,𝒜,p,r⟩​with​r​(s,a,s′)=σ​(s,a,s′)⊤​wr}\mathcal{D}\mathrel{\doteq}\{{M}|{M}=\langle\
 mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p,r\rangle\ \text{with}\ r(s,a,s^{\prime})=\sigma(s,a,s^{\prime})^{\top}w_
 {r}\}

with σ:𝒮×𝒜×𝒮↦Σ\sigma:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}\mapsto\Sigma and wr∈Rdw_{r}\in\
mathbb{R}^{d} specific to each task M{M}.

We can now rely on outcomes to describe domains in terms of domain-invariant commonalities.
However, they only help us to describe transitions relevant for the one-step reward function. Many
transitions are unremarkable and have no non-zero outcomes associated with them. To overcome this
lack of descriptive power we can talk about a sequence of outcomes allowing us to go beyond a
single time step: specifically, a sequence of ouctomes given a sequence of actions and a starting
state. Since such an outcome sequence is conditional on future behavior (an action sequence), this
gives us a way to describe the future of a state in terms of outcomes. We formalize the following.

Definition 3.3 (outcome sequence–test).

Let M=⟨𝒮,𝒜,p,r⟩{M}=\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p,r\rangle be an MDP and let σ⁡(s,a,S′)\sigma
(s,a,S^{\prime}) be the random variable describing the distribution over outcomes associated with
the transition from state ss following action aa, with S′S^{\prime} the random variable describing
