5.2 The Craftworld Domain

Refer to caption


Figure 13: Left: depiction of a Craftworld task. The diagram on the right illustrates crafting
dependencies.

The first environment is based on the Craftworld introduced by Andreas et al. (2017). It is
implemented in VGDL^2^2 2 VGDL 2.0: a Video Game Description Language (https://github.com/
rubenvereecken/py-vgdl) as the framework allows easy generation of new tasks due to simple
plain-text task specifications that can be automatically generated. An agent is randomly placed in
a grid where it needs to craft an end product. To do so, it must gather resources and refine these
at their corresponding workstations. It can pick up resources by walking over them and interact
with an appropriate workstation by facing it and performing a use action.

We restrict ourselves to one Craftworld domain where the agent’s end goal is to craft a hammer,
first creating the intermediate handle and hammer head. One can see in the example depicted in
Figure 13 that each task has at least two optimal sequences of actions since subtasks can be
completed in any order.

An agent is to complete its objective in as few steps as possible. To this end it receives a
penalty of -1 on each action taken, except its final action which is rewarded with 1000. We
restrict ourselves to 4 by 4 grids.

5.2.1 Types of Agent

We use two types of reinforcement learning agents: a regular agent that has access to primitive
actions only, referred to as the option-less agent, and an option-enabled, hierarchical agent that
in addition to primitive actions can also initiate options. Agents use SARSA(λ\lambda) with ϵ\
epsilon-greedy policies. We fixed ϵ=0.05\epsilon=0.05, λ=0.99\lambda=0.99, γ=0.999\gamma=0.999 and
set the learning rate to 0.20.2, all as results of a hyper-parameter sweep.

5.2.2 State Representation

The base state representation is a fully Markov state representation where each state is encoded
separately: a one-hot representation which we will denote x⁡(s)x(s). This is used by the
option-less agent as well as the high-level policy of the hierarchical agent.

The option’s state representation is an OPSR ϕσ,k​(s)\phi_{\sigma,k}(s) as defined in Equation 25.
This leaves us to define the outcome function σ\sigma and a maximal action sequence length kk. We
will define outcomes for all changes in resource counts in order to capture every time the agent
gains or loses a resource (by crafting it into another one). Using resource​(s)\texttt{resource}(s)
to denote the number of resources of type resource the agent has in state ss, and defining the
short-hand Δ​resource≐resource​(s′)−resource​(s)\Delta\texttt{resource}\mathrel{\doteq}\texttt
{resource}(s^{\prime})~-~\texttt{resource}(s), we can write the outcome function for our Craftworld
domain as

 σ⁡(s,a,s′)=(Δ​wood,Δ​stone,Δ​handle,Δ​head,Δ​hammer)\sigma(s,a,s^{\prime})=(\Delta\texttt{wood},\
 Delta\texttt{stone},\Delta\texttt{handle},\Delta\texttt{head},\Delta\texttt{hammer})

This will capture what is relevant to reward as well as express information that we think could be
relevant to options.

5.2.3 Option Discovery

We derive traces from training tasks using Policy Iteration. All control policies and option
termination policies are parameterised by single layer neural networks with a softmax activation.
The high-level policy is denoted πH\pi_{H}.

 πH​(s)\displaystyle\pi_{H}(s)   =softmax​(W​x​(s))\displaystyle=\textit{softmax}(Wx(s))
 πω​(s)\displaystyle\pi_{\omega} =softmax(Wϕσ,1:k(s)+b)\displaystyle=\textit{softmax}(W\phi_{\
 (s)                             sigma,1:k}(s)+b)
 βω​(s)\displaystyle\beta_{\     =softmax(Wϕσ,1:k(s)+b)\displaystyle=\textit{softmax}(W\phi_{\
 omega}(s)                       sigma,1:k}(s)+b)

Calculation of the gradient is outlined in Appendix B while stepping of the gradient was performed
with the Adam algorithm (Kingma and Ba, 2014) using a learning rate of 0.30.3. Trajectories were
sampled randomly from the training set, one epoch seeing each trajectory once. Learning continued
until the joint likelihood of the traces reached 0.990.99 or until convergence.

To reduce the size of the sizeable prediction-based state spaces, we first apply principal
component analysis and keep only those features that together explain 99% of variance in the
training set.

5.2.4 Experimental Structure

We generated 100 Craftworld tasks of size 4 by 4, all with the same structure as outlined in
Figure 13. To evaluate performance of our option-enabled agent on a task, we ran option discovery
10 times on a single trace each of the remaining 99 tasks. For each option discovery outcome, we
train the option-enabled agent 10 times for 100 episodes each, totalling 100 samples per target
task. Results are averaged over all 100 target tasks, totalling 10000 samples per result.

5.2.5 Evaluation: Expressivity of Abstraction

Refer to caption
Figure 14: Learning curves for an agent without options and agents with three discovered OPSR ϕσ,k\
phi_{\sigma,k} options, with kk ranging from 1 to 4. A higher accumulated reward is better.

What is the impact of the expressivity of our chosen state abstraction? In other words, how much
information should we include? The fewer expected outcomes we include in our state abstraction, the
coarser it is, the more states get aggregated and the more we can reuse behavior, yet the less
information is available to base the behavior on. We investigate this question by varying the
parameter kk in ϕσ,k\phi_{\sigma,k}.

Figure 14 shows accumulated reward per epoch for the primitive agent as well as for 4 settings of
the option-enabled agent, corresponding to k∈{1,2,3,4}k\in\{1,2,3,4\}. It also shows the learning
curve of an agent without access to options. The option-less agent is outperformed by each
instantiation of our option-enabled agent, with a learning curve that is shallower at each epoch.
Even options based on an OPSR with k=1k=1 do better, from which we can conclude that even a very
restricted state abstraction can be expressive and general enough to learn transferable behavior
on.

The learning curve of the setting with k=3k=3 outperforms any other one at each epoch.
Interestingly, the setting k=4k=4 shows decreased performance. There appears to be a trade-off
between complexity and performance, where past a certain threshold more complex representations
incur penalties. Of course, considering action sequences of length k=4k=4 adds four times as many
features. We think this increase in features resulted in more distinct states, which require more
learning. At a given point, there simply is a need for more data, i.e. demonstrated trajectories.

5.2.6 Evaluation: Number of Options


Figure 15: Craftworld for OPSR 1-3.

Since we pose no restrictions on the behavior of options aside from the bias implicit in
parameterisation, it is interesting to investigate the different behaviors emerging with different
amounts of options in play. To describe the performance of an agent we can condense and summarize a
single learning curve by simply looking at the area under that curve. We can then look at the
distribution of areas under the reward curve (AURC) across runs.

Figure 15 shows such distributions of AURC, one for each setting, each setting corresponding to a
different number of options. It shows that even a single discovered option can boost learning as
compared to learning without options. Increasing the number of options further helps learning up to
a point where it saturates. There exists a trade-off in using options: options encode useful and
reusable behavior yet come at the cost of a larger action space for the high-level agent, which in
turn requires more learning to handle. This is exactly the trade-off we discussed earlier in
section 4.

5.2.7 Option Trace Inspection

We have shown that our options help speed up learning, yet it is unclear how exactly they are
helping. The outcome-based abstraction is complex and hard to interpret and an option’s behavior is
learned entirely from scratch. In order to gain some insight in how exactly options helped, we ran
option discovery once for 44 options with an OPSR with k=3k=3. We then want to investigate how this
set of learned options tends to be used across multiple runs of learning an option-enabled policy.
In particular, we want to investigate whether these options implemented generic all-purpose
behavior that would be useful anywhere, or whether they implemented more specific behavior for a
particular subtask that the option-enabled agent could rely on at the right time. In order to test
this, we trained an option-enabled agent to completion on the Craftworld task depicted in Figure 13
, resulting in one final, ideally optimal trace. We repeated this a number of times to account for
randomness, seeing if different behaviors emerged given the same set of options.

In order to investigate the resulting trajectories we devised a method to extract trajectories that
are somehow similar so we do not compare option use between wildly different trajectories. To this
end we define a trajectory’s story as the sequence of non-zero outcomes in that trajectory. For our
investigation of option use it is sufficient that we look at trajectories with the same story. We
extracted the stories for all final trajectories, then randomly selected 100100 trajectories that
converged to the dominant story, each from a different option-enabled agent that was trained from
scratch. The dominant story for this set of options was (stone,head,wood,handle,hammer)(\texttt
{stone},\texttt{head},\texttt{wood},\texttt{handle},\texttt{hammer}), i.e. crafting the hammer head
first, then the handle from scratch.

For each trajectory, we looked at which option was in use at which time step. Figure 16 combines
all 100100 trajectories by showing for each time step how many out of the 100 trajectories spent
that time step in which option. We refer to the resulting graph as the option occupancy graph. The
optimal trajectory consists of 1414 steps, which is why the count drops significantly from time
step 1515 onwards, with the longest trajectory taking 24 time steps.


Figure 16: Option occupancy for 100100 traces following the same story. πH\pi_{H} denotes when the
high-level controller picks a primitive actions instead of an option.

There are some clear patterns in Figure 16. Option ω4\omega_{4} seems to be clearly useful at the
start of the task, with nearly 60 trajectories spending the first time step in option ω4\omega_{4}.
It is followed by options ω1\omega_{1} or ω2\omega_{2} (and, on closer manual inspection of
individual trajectories, sometimes both). This interesting splitting of behaviors turned out to be
due to the multiple equally optimal ways the agent can navigate from one point to the next, despite
following the same story. Upon inspection, each of ω1\omega_{1} and ω2\omega_{2} appeared to deal
with one such an optimal route. Option ω3\omega_{3} was used more towards the end of the
trajectory, though so was the primitive behavior which appeared to be preferable to options during
that period of an agent’s lifetime.

If options did not specialize behavior and instead were useful everywhere, we would expect to see a
very uniform option occupancy graph with each color roughly equally present at each time step. The
option occupancy graph looks far from uniform. Seeing the same patterns reoccur in differently
initialized option-enabled agents demonstrates that each option implemented a certain behavior that
was useful for different parts of the greater task.

When we re-ran option discovery with different seeds, quite different option behaviors emerged.
This was apparent from the dominant story being different (yet, again, optimal), along with very
different option occupancy graphs. This is because option discovery is an under-constrained
problem; many discovered option sets fit the demonstrated trajectories equally well. This is
necessary by design; by limiting expressiveness, we prevent options from overfitting on particular
traces, ensuring that they are reusable across tasks.

5.3 The Lightworld Domain

The Lightworld domain was conceived by Konidaris and Barto (2007) who tailored it to demonstrate
their theory of agent-space representations. We treat it here because of the similarity with our
work. An agent-space representation is quite loosely defined as an abstraction that captures local
information centered on the agent. An agent-space abstraction should be engineered to be coarser
than a fully Markov representation for the same reason we employ coarse OPSRs: to enable reusable
behavior across a task. In fact, agent-spaces shine when the space is the same across tasks so
behavior can be reused across tasks. From this description outcome-predictive representations are
themselves agent-space representations, as they are by definition centered on the agent; expected
outcome sequences are based on action sequences that always start from the agent’s current state.
Since agent-spaces are fully engineered, predictive representations can be seen as a generalisation
that allows for a more principled and even automatic construction of agent-spaces.

A Lightworld task exists of a number of rooms in succession. Exiting the final room means success.
Aside from doors a room can contain keys and locks. Some rooms do not contain keys, in which case
the lock can be opened without them. Keys are picked up by navigating over them and employing a 
pickup action. Locks require an unlock action.

Refer to caption
Figure 17: A Lightworld task with three rooms, implemented in VGDL 2.0. The same task description
can be found in (Konidaris and Barto, 2007).

5.3.1 State Representations

We again employ a one-hot Markov state representation for both the option-less and the
option-enabled learner. For options we consider two different state abstraction constructions: the
agent-centered representation described by Konidaris and Barto (2007) and our prediction-based
OPSR.

Konidaris and Barto (2007) consider the agent as having sensors which can sense notable things in
its environment. The three objects that can be sensed are key, lock, and door. Sensors only work in
a straight line and encode their findings as a value ranging from 0 to 1; 0 when 20 tiles away and
1 when on top. Given the four cardinal directions this results in 12 values.

Our Lightworld OPSR is based on the following outcome function:

 σ⁡(s,a,s′)=(Δ​room,Δ​key,Δ​door,Δ​goal)\sigma(s,a,s^{\prime})=(\Delta\texttt{room},\Delta\texttt
 {key},\Delta\texttt{door},\Delta\texttt{goal})

5.3.2 Experimental Structure

We closely follow the experimental protocol where Lightworld was first conceived to allow for a
fair comparison between approaches. We randomly generated 100 Lightworld tasks with 2 to 5 rooms
each, each room 5 to 15 tiles wide and high, and each containing a key with probability 1/31/3. The
protocol to generate samples is the same as before.

5.3.3 Evaluation: Agent-Space and OPSR Options

We investigate discovered options for agent-space and outcome-based state abstractions. In both
cases we only show results for 4 options as that was the optimal number of options according to a
parameter sweep (different from the 3 predefined options in (Konidaris and Barto, 2007)). Figure 18
presents results for target tasks with differing numbers of rooms. Note that even though
performance is grouped by the number of rooms in the target task, options were still learned from
all other tasks regardless of the number of rooms.

In smaller tasks with only two rooms, using either type of option does not improve learning. It is
only as tasks grow more difficult (with more rooms) that the benefit of options outweights their
cost, with tasks consisting of five rooms benefiting the most. Interestingly, our OPSR-based
options outperformed options based on the manually engineered agent-space, despite the agent-space
describing longer action sequences and so having information about more steps into the future. It
appears that OPSRs capture essential local information that benefits reusable options, more so than
a state abstraction designed by an expert. Looking at the setting with 5 rooms, OPSR-based
discovered options also constitute the only approach that nets a positive return on average, with
the option-less agent evidently struggling. These reusable options appear crucial for tackling a
task of only moderate complexity.


Figure 18: Distribution of areas under the reward curve for target Lightworld tasks with different
numbers of rooms. Three settings are shown: without options, with discovered OPSR options and with
discovered agent-space options.

5.3.4 Evaluation: Transfer Between Subdomains


Figure 19: Distribution of areas under the reward curve in the Lightworld domain with transfer
between and within subdomains. Results labeled ‘extra subdomain’ have always been learned from
tasks with only 2 rooms, whereas results labeled ‘intra subdomain’ have been learned from tasks
with the same number of rooms as indicated.

The Lightworld domain consists of tasks made up of 2 to 5 rooms. We could say that all tasks with
the same number of rooms belong to the same subdomain. This raises the question: how well does
transfer work between different subdomains? That is, given demonstrated trajectories from one
subdomain, how well do the options learned from these trajectories perform on tasks from another
subdomain?

We have decided to look into this question using options learned from tasks with 2 rooms, evaluated
on tasks with 3 to 5 rooms, (extra-subdomain transfer). This is more than simple transfer between
subdomains; a task with more rooms is decidedly more complicated than its room-deficient cousins,
as can be seen from the results of the previous experiment in Figure 18. This allows us to
investigate whether options learned in simpler tasks perform in more complicated tasks.

Figure 19 shows the performances of extra- against intra-subdomain transfer. The latter refers to
the case where options are learned from tasks with the same number of rooms. It is clear that
extra-subdomain transfer works: options learned in simpler tasks are useful in more complicated
tasks, despite never having encountered one before. What is more, options learned from a simpler
subdomain almost match the performance of options learned within the same, more complicated
subdomain, as can be seen from comparing extra-subdomain with intra-subdomain transfer boxplots.

These results point at the interesting possibility of curriculum learning. To solve a complicated
task where one has access to simpler ones, one could learn options in progressively harder tasks in
a curriculum fashion, each set of options making the next subdomain easier to tackle.

6 Discussion of Related Work

6.1 Transfer Through State Abstraction

Transfer Setting

Transfer is hard and generally not very well understood in the context of reinforcement learning.
One indicator of this is the lack of a common and concise language for describing a transfer
setting, for example in terms of the allowed differences between tasks. Some papers allow
differences only in the reward dynamics (e.g. goal location; (Barreto et al., 2017)), others only
changes in what might be referred to as layout while keeping the (loosely termed) dynamics the
same, and others again only slight changes in the dynamics (e.g. actuator strength; (Yang et al.,
2020; Barekatain et al., 2020; Lee et al., 2020a)). All of the above examples are cast under the
same nomer of transfer and it can be a considerable undertaking to figure out the transfer setting
in a particular work. We preface with this because our area of transfer is a fairly general one,
with OPSRs working across different transition dynamics, layout, and even reward dynamics.

Underlying Transfer Method

All transfer is by definition the result of some mapping, implicit or otherwise. This paper has
focused on transfer through state abstraction, where the mapping is an equivalence relation
resulting from states sharing an abstraction or between abstraction and ground states. This mapping
can be relaxed – many papers achieve partial transfer through similarity (rather than equivalence)
based on some (often implicit) measure (see also discussion in Section 2.2). We found that many
papers that we will discuss shortly rely on state abstraction (or representation) to achieve
transfer so we will not discuss in detail less common transfer approaches based on other types of
mapping (e.g. the task mappings of (Taylor et al., 2007)). We also largely focus on transfer in
model-free RL rather than model-based RL (Tirinzoni et al., 2020; Winder et al., 2020; Perez et
al., 2020).

State Abstraction per se

Knowledge transfer in reinforcement learning through state abstraction is not a particularly new
notion (Andre and Russell, 2002), relying on a long tradition of abstraction in planning (Amarel,
1981). State abstraction tends to be seen as state aggregation (Li et al., 2006), as the removal of
distinguishing features, two states appearing the same as a result. This traditional view still
stands, and can for example be found in the more recent work of Akrour et al. on robot manipulation
(Akrour et al., 2018) and Abel et al. on lifelong learning (Abel et al., 2016; Abel et al., 2018).
Finally, Frommberger and Wolter describe different types of abstraction and stress that abstraction
is not merely about reducing data, but also about focusing on relevant information (Frommberger and
Wolter, 2010).

Agent-Space and Deictic Representations

Our state abstractions that describe possible behaviors hark back to the deictic (roughly meaning 
“centered on the agent”) representations of Agre and Chapman, who realized that ego-centric state
representations capture general concepts (Agre and Chapman, 1987). Konidaris and Barto picked up
this idea again and termed their own agent-centric state abstractions agent-space representations (
Konidaris and Barto, 2007). While agent-spaces are useful for transfer, their problem lies with
their manual engineering (Konidaris et al., 2012). Problems aside, task-agnostic and sufficiently
coarse, agent-spaces perfectly capture what it takes to transfer beneficially. It is for this
reason that we think that so much related work has implicitly opted for agent-space–like
representations that they ultimally owe their ability for transfer to (Chalmers et al., 2018;
Ferret et al., 2020). Others have attempted to learn shared representations which inevitably must
have commonalities with agent-spaces (Roy and Konidaris, 2020; Wu et al., 2020).

Successor Representations

While perhaps not its own category of representation, successor representations (originally by (
Dayan, 1993)) deserve their own discussion for the frenzy of related work that they sparked. In the
paper that blew new life into the idea, Barreto et al. introduced successor features,
representations (themselves learned on top of a given agent-space–like representation) that enabled
transfer between tasks with the same transition dynamics but different reward dynamics by learning
a value function independent of the task’s reward weighting (Barreto et al., 2017; Barreto et al.,
2018). Since then, the framework has been insightfully compared to model-based RL (Lehnert and
Littman, 2019), had its applicability for transfer extended (Madarasz, 2019), and been extended
with options (Ramesh et al., 2019; Barreto et al., 2019).

Relational Representations

A traditional idea that has recently gained more traction again is that relational representations
are required for generalisation (Dzeroski et al., 2001). It has even been used to achieve
transferable options (Croonenborghs et al., 2007). Hamrick et al. (2018) concluded that a 
relational inductive bias is elementary for generalisation in experiments not unlike our own. This
premise is for example explored by (Zambaldi et al., 2019) who implemented it in a deep learning
context, though the authors note that it is not yet fully understood why relational representations
afford generalisation. Also relying on graph networks, Garg et al. (2020) learned generalized
policies for relational MDPs, expressing their hopes for the future of relational representations.
We posit that all relational representations are predictive in nature and suspect that they can be
translated into our framework of outcomes. In the example of the Box World of (Zambaldi et al.,
2019) where the agent needs to open a sequence of boxes with corresponding keys, a high weight
between a key and a box corresponds to the outcome of the chest opening after picking up the key
and walking up to the box.

Predictive Representations

There have been multiple avenues of work pursuing predictive representations in RL. Littman et al.
sparked an interesting series of research on Predictive State Representations in Partially
Observable MDP’s (Littman et al., 2002; Singh et al., 2004). Based on the idea of tests (Rivest and
Schapire, 1993), they represented a state in a POMDP in terms of predictions of observations given
a history of observations. These representations were sufficient to describe the underlying hidden
states. Though different from our setting in the task descriptions and the use of observations, 
Rafols et al. (2005) showed that PSRs enabled transfer and even showed that there was a trade-off
in the expressivity of the representation and the benefit to learning. PSRs have even been extended
with options (Wolfe and Singh, 2006) and combined with relational knowledge (Wingate et al., 2007).
PSRs found new life through the spectral learning approaches of Boots (culminating in their thesis;
2012), which still makes up an active area of research (Kulesza et al., 2015).

In an originally different avenue of work, Sutton (2009) claims that all knowledge is predictive in
nature. Sutton et al. (2011) later continue this idea in a practical setting where a robot learns
to predict sensorimotor signals as general value functions. Sutton and Tanner build on this and
investigate whether the approach can be used to learn PSRs, with promising results (Sutton and
Tanner, 2015). Modayil et al. linked this work to nexting (Modayil et al., 2014), the phenomenon of
humans and animals continually making a large number of predictions about what happens around them
on multiple timescales, and demonstrated a scalable way to implement the same phenomenon in a
robot. Since the purpose of nexting is not yet entirely understood, we suppose that nexting could
be the mechanism through which animals represent abstract concepts, in line with the claims of 
Sutton (2009).

Leveraging predictions for state representations is still a very active area of research to this
day (Misra et al., 2020; Guo et al., 2020; Schwarzer et al., 2021; Lee et al., 2020b). It is
because of this entire body of work regarding predictive representations that we are optimistic
about the feasibility of learning OPSRs.

Affordances

Finally, there has been some interesting work on affordances, originally defined by Gibson as the
possible interactions an organism can detect in its environment (Gibson, 1977). Despite
inconsistencies in the ontological use of the term in the literature (Dohn, 2006; Bonderup Dohn,
2009), work on affordances generally tackles the question “What can I do here?” (Khetarpal et al.,
2020). Perhaps unsurprisingly then, the concept of affordances has inspired work in rich
environments requiring both navigation and interaction (Abel et al., 2014; Qi et al., 2020;
Nagarajan and Grauman, 2020). We discuss affordances in the context of our work because OPSRs
capture what is possible in a particular state by capturing all future outcomes. We believe that
there is a deep connection between predictive representations and affordances – one that merits
more discussion.

Multi-Task Representation Learning

Finally, multi-task representation learning does not subscribe to a particular view and instead
tries to learn good (shared) state representations in a setting where one has access to a
distribution over tasks or even to both a source and target task (Maurer et al., 2016; Ruder, 2017;
Zhang et al., 2019; Ben-David et al., 2010). This approach relies on a considerable amount of given
information which is not always feasible. The well-defined setting does give rise to interesting
theoretical investigations into using representations that are shared across tasks (Wu et al., 2020
; D’Eramo et al., 2020; Du et al., 2020).

6.2 Hierarchical Reinforcement Learning

HRL per se

The main goal currently under investigation in hierarchical reinforcement learning, i.e. RL relying
on action abstraction, is to make longer and more complex tasks more tractable by virtue of task
decomposition (be it through options, skills, abstract actions, macros, etc) (Wen et al., 2020).
One way HRL delivers on this is targeted exploration (Jinnai et al., 2020a; Jinnai et al., 2020b).
Another way is more consistent, targeted action that aims to reach subgoals, an endeavour that has
sparked a series of work in subgoal discovery (Machado et al., 2017; Nair and Finn, 2019; Goyal et
al., 2019; Jurgenson et al., 2020; Wen et al., 2020) and even subgoal direction discovery (
Vezhnevets et al., 2017; Nachum et al., 2019). While abstract actions are usually combined
temporally, some work also exists on composite actions, leading to a different kind of hierarchy (
Peng et al., 2019; Tian et al., 2020).

State Abstraction in HRL

One part of any HRL approach is the state space that the abstract action operates over. It is
possible to simply use the original state space, the same one the high-level agent uses (Nachum et
al., 2018). Alternatively, end-to-end approaches that learn both high-level and low-level agents
(abstract actions) can simultaneously learn representations for the abstract actions to operate
over (Bacon et al., 2017; Vezhnevets et al., 2017). These option-specific representations can be
seen as state abstractions. In contrast with previous work, Nachum et al. learn the low-level
representation from unsupervised objectives (Nachum et al., 2019) This representation transfers to
different tasks, although this representation transfer does not result in policy transfer. Of
special interest to us, this work also investigates sub-optimality of a representation when used in
a hierarchical setting. Other approaches to low-level state representations include grounding in
language (Jiang et al., 2019) and relying on concealed information (Vezhnevets et al., 2020).

Transfer in HRL

Finally there is the holy grail, a long-standing dream for HRL: reusing abstract actions in other
tasks (Barto et al., 2013). Or, in the common case where state abstraction is used as the
underlying method: combining state abstraction and action abstraction (Konidaris, 2019). Most of
the HRL approaches discussed so far relied on task-specific representations and as such did not
transfer to new tasks. The insight that state representation is the limiting factor led to the
early proposal of agent-space–based options by Konidaris and Barto (Konidaris and Barto, 2007;
Konidaris et al., 2012), though their agent-spaces (task-agnostic representations) were engineered
and their options partially pre-defined. Recent approaches try to learn both a task-agnostic state
abstraction and the transferable abstract action on top of that. One such a line of enquiry focuses
on object-centric representations (Topin et al., 2015; Zadaianchuk et al., 2020) which are again
reminiscent of agent-spaces. Modhe et al. achieve transfer of options by manually introducing
partial observability (and therefore state aliasing through coarsening) in state abstractions.
Others attempt to learn a suitable state representation from a source domain which tends to need
adaptation in a target task (Frans et al., 2018; Peng et al., 2019; Qureshi et al., 2020).

Outcome-predictive state representations and the theory behind them give an answer to the question
of what makes a state representation useful for transfer, taking away the need to rely on
agent-space–like engineering. What’s more, combined with options they also give an answer to the
big question of how to make HRL work for transfer as we have demonstrated. Finally, work on
learning transferable state representations for HRL is very recent and we believe that the insights
around OPSRs can guide this area of research in the right direction.

6.3 A Cognitive Perspective and Motivation

Wheeler and Clark (1999) argue that much of complicated animal behavior emerges from a complicated
interplay between animal and environment, where often this behavior is attributed only to the
animal’s “intelligence”, its cognitive functions. This phenomenon of over-attribution has been
termed causal spread by Wheeler and Clark (1999). In this paper we heed the caution of causal
spread and advocate for a shift away from the traditional agent that simply interfaces with the
world in favor of an agent that is fully embedded in the world (an embodied, embedded mind, (Clark,
2008)). In the traditional cartesian view the agent perceives its environment, reconstructs an
internal representation, and only then leverages this representation for action (Wheeler, 2005). In
contrast we advocate for perception that is heavily geared towards (and guided by) action
primarily, where perceiving an object for example does not happen in terms of objective properties
but rather in terms of how to negotiate that object.

Clark (1997) discusses a robot which employs a map that is encoded in terms of percepts and
actions. The map is prescriptive, it describes locations in terms of which movements would get the
robot to different locations and the robot has no planning module outside the map – the map is
itself the controller. Clark coined the term action-oriented representations to describe
representations of this nature (Clark, 1997, p. 47-51). An action-oriented representation of an
object, instead of describing the object, immediately encodes how to negotiate the object. Closely
related is Gibson’s notion of affordances, which are possible interactions an organism can detect
in its environment relative to its own skills (Gibson, 1977). In later work, Clark describes
perception as fundamentally predictive (Clark, 2014), delivering action-oriented representations
that, once modulated, prescribe how to act and respond – that is, representations of affordances (
Clark, 2016). This would be great: both predictions and affordances have been thought to be great
basis for generalizability because…

In our example of unlocking a door, an action-oriented representation of a lock is nothing so
descriptive as a brass rectangle with a keyhole, but perhaps as fitting a key – the key itself as 
unlocking the door or as fitting the lock. Now, how can an agent know in advance that a sequence of
primitive actions will lead to a complicated behavior such as opening a lock? It needs to be
capable of postulating goals. Rather than the agent imagining complicated future states, we follow
the caution of causal spread (of over-attributing intelligence to an agent), and instead we think
it is the environment which signals to the agent when something has succeeded, signals what is
relevant to the agent’s subsequent behavior and its goals. When turning a key in its lock we do not
model the ‘openness’ of the door, be it as a percept or otherwise, instead we simply anticipate a
click of the lock or a jolt of the door. Anticipating select signals is far cheaper than complex
internal modelling and, perhaps more importantly, constitutes a kind of universal knowledge. All
locks click or jolt when opened, their shapes or sizes matter not.

To formalize this we introduce outcomes, cues derived from transitions in the environment that are
important to the agent. This is not to be taken too literally: the environment does not actively
signal the agent. Instead the agent perceives outcomes in transitions of the environment because
they are relevant to the agent. Example outcomes are “the key slides in the lock” or “the lock
clicks open”. Outcomes are derived from the agent’s perception of these events. They tend to signal
the enabling of future behavior: the key being in the lock allows turning the key, the lock
clicking allows one to successfully turn the door handle. Importantly, outcomes form a domain-wide
language which allows us to talk about state transitions in a way that is completely independent of
the underlying task-specific state. They capture what is really important for a particular goal and
ignore what is extraneous. They form the base of our abstractions.

To achieve an action-oriented representation like that of Clark (1997) we represent states in terms
of the future outcomes they afford. That is, we represent states in terms of predictions of what
outcomes follow, following different short term behaviors. To take our example, there are a few
combinations of muscular movements that would successfully enter a key into a lock, quite a few
more than would result in missing the lock and perhaps some that end in scratching the door. Put
together, the representation of a key becomes the union of all these predicted outcomes following
different behaviors. We term this representation an Outcome-Predictive State Representation (OPSR).
It ticks all the boxes of the previous narrative: an action-oriented, predictive representation
which captures affordances.

In this paper, we both argue and demonstrate that it is possible to leverage this representation to
learn transferable skills. Two sets of locks and keys may visually look very different, yet look
very similar if we look at them in terms of predicted outcomes. It is this aggregation of distinct
situations under a shared representations that makes OPSR an abstraction. This combined state and
action abstraction (Konidaris, 2019) is key to this paper.

7 Conclusion and Future Work

This paper contains both theoretical and practical contributions on transfer in Reinforcement
Learning. We first introduce a strong theoretical framework based on state abstractions that
describes the quality of a transferred policy (transfer value) in both single- and multi-task
settings. Based on these insights we propose a new state abstraction based on predictions of
reward-relevant changes in the environment (outcomes), termed Outcome-Predictive State
Representation (OPSR). Since outcomes describe transitions in a task-agnostic manner, OPSRs equally
form a task-agnostic state description which naturally leads to transfer. We show that OPSRs are
particularly useful because of their bottom-up construction which automatically leads to transfer
optimality in a class of MDPs which we term plannable.

Next, we demonstrate how predicting fewer outcomes coarsens the state abstraction, leading to state
aliasing and increased opportunity for transfer. While coarsening normally incurs a penalty in 
transfer value, subtasks can offer the best of both worlds. We introduce OPSR-based skills that
transfer between tasks as a means to leverage both state abstraction and action abstraction to
achieve transfer. In a series of experiments conducted with a new library for transfer research
(VGDL 2.0), we show that OPSR-based options can be fully learned in one set of tasks and
subsequently speed up learning in new and unseen tasks with zero processing.

The practical investigation in this paper is a proof of concept of the usefulness of OPSRs that
relies on two assumptions: both outcomes and their predictions are available to the agent. The next
step left for future work is to learn both of these. The work in related areas (as discussed in
Section 6) and the observation that outcomes are far more parsimonious goal representations than
fully concrete future states make us confident in the feasability of this step. In addition, human
brains appear to heavily limit predictions according to needs and available actions (König et al.,
2013); such limitation would greatly improve tractability. Another extension is to realize an agent
that learns OPSR-based skills in an online fashion rather than offline after episodes. This would
allow the options to not only help with transfer but also solving complex tasks. Finally, it would
be interesting to consider multiple levels in the hierarchy of skills. Instead of sequences of
actions after which outcomes are predicted we would have sequences of options, resulting in a
high-level OPSR that enables transfer at the higher level in addition to the lower level.

Appendix A Proofs

Theorem 2.1.

Proof (2.1).

Let M=⟨𝒮,𝒜,p,r⟩{M}=\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p,r\rangle be an MDP and let ϕ:𝒮↦Φ\phi:\mathcal
{S}\mapsto\Phi be a state abstraction that maps two states s,t∈𝒮s,t\in\mathcal{S} to the same
equivalence class ϕ¯\bar{\phi}. We will prove (32) by contradiction in two parts, one for each
direction of the biconditional.

(⇒)(\bm{\Rightarrow})

We proceed with a proof by contradiction. Assume that ∃s,t∈𝒮\exists s,t\in\mathcal{S} such that
both of the following hold:

 ϕ⁡(s)=ϕ⁡(t)\displaystyle\phi(s)=\phi(t)                                                       (27)
 ∃δ∈Πδ(ϕ,M):δ(⋅∣s)≠δ(⋅∣t)\displaystyle\exists\delta\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,{M}):\delta(\cdot\mid  (28)
 s)\neq\delta(\cdot\mid t)

Any abstract policy πϕ:Φ↦𝒫⁡(𝒜)\pi_{\phi}:\Phi\mapsto\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A}) will have the same
distribution over actions for the abstract state ϕ⁡(s)=ϕ⁡(t)\phi(s)=\phi(t):

 πϕ(⋅∣ϕ(s))=πϕ(⋅∣ϕ(t))\pi_{\phi}(\cdot\mid\phi(s))=\pi_{\phi}(\cdot\mid\phi(t))

By definition of derived policies along with the assumption in (27) we have

 δ(⋅∣s)=πϕ(⋅∣ϕ(s))=πϕ(⋅∣ϕ(t))=δ(⋅∣t)\delta(\cdot\mid s)=\pi_{\phi}(\cdot\mid\phi(s))=\pi_{\    (29)
 phi}(\cdot\mid\phi(t))=\delta(\cdot\mid t)

This is in contradiction with (28) which proves the forward implication.

(⇐)(\bm{\Leftarrow})

We again proceed with a proof by contradiction. Assume that ∃s,t∈𝒮\exists s,t\in\mathcal{S} such
that:

 ϕ⁡(s)≠ϕ⁡(t)\displaystyle\phi(s)\neq\phi(t)                                                    (30)
 ∀δ∈Πδ(ϕ,M):δ(⋅∣s)=δ(⋅∣t)\displaystyle\forall\delta\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,{M}):\delta(\cdot\mid  (31)
 s)=\delta(\cdot\mid t)

Consider an abstract policy πϕ:Φ↦𝒫⁡(𝒜)\pi_{\phi}:\Phi\mapsto\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A}) which assigns
different distributions over actions for abstract states ϕ⁡(s)\phi(s) and ϕ⁡(t)\phi(t):

 πϕ(⋅∣ϕ(s))≠πϕ(⋅∣ϕ(t))\pi_{\phi}(\cdot\mid\phi(s))\neq\pi_{\phi}(\cdot\mid\phi(t))

By definition of derived policies along with the assumption in (30) we have

 δ(⋅∣s)=πϕ(⋅∣ϕ(s))≠πϕ(⋅∣ϕ(s))=δ(⋅∣s)\begin{gathered}\delta(\cdot\mid s)=\pi_{\phi}(\cdot\mid\phi
 (s))\neq\pi_{\phi}(\cdot\mid\phi(s))=\delta(\cdot\mid s)\end{gathered}

This is in contradiction with (31) which proves the reverse implication.

∎

Theorem 2.2.

Let M=⟨𝒮,𝒜,p,r⟩{M}=\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p,r\rangle be an MDP and let ϕ:𝒮↦Φ\phi:\mathcal
{S}\mapsto\Phi be a state abstraction that maps two states s,t∈𝒮s,t\in\mathcal{S} to the same
equivalence class ϕ¯\bar{\phi}. Then all derived policies for ϕ\phi must have the same
distributions over actions for both ss and tt. Formally, ∀s,t∈𝒮:\forall s,t\in\mathcal{S}:

 ϕ(s)=ϕ(t)⇔∀δ∈Πδ(ϕ,M):δ(⋅∣s)=δ(⋅∣t)\phi(s)=\phi(t)\;\Leftrightarrow\;\forall\delta\in\Pi_{\    (32)
 delta}(\phi,{M}):\delta(\cdot\mid s)=\delta(\cdot\mid t)

Proof (2.2).

Let M=⟨𝒮,𝒜,p,r⟩{M}=\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p,r\rangle be an MDP and let ϕ:𝒮↦Φ\phi:\mathcal
{S}\mapsto\Phi and ϕ′:𝒮↦Φ′\phi^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\Phi^{\prime} be two state abstractions.
We prove (7) by proving its inverse instead:

 ϕ′⋡ϕ⟺Πδ​(ϕ′,M)⊉Πδ​(ϕ,M)\begin{gathered}\phi^{\prime}\not\succeq\phi\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\   (33)
 Pi_{\delta}(\phi^{\prime},{M})\not\supseteq\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,{M})\end{gathered}

Or, more explictitly:

 ∃δ∈Πδ​(ϕ,M):δ∉Πδ​(ϕ′,M)⇕∃s,t∈𝒮:ϕ⁡(s)≠ϕ⁡(t)∧ϕ′​(s)=ϕ′​(t)\begin{gathered}\exists\delta\in\Pi_
 {\delta}(\phi,{M}):\delta\notin\Pi_{\delta}(\phi^{\prime},{M})\\ \Updownarrow\\ \exists s,t\  (34)
 in\mathcal{S}:\phi(s)\neq\phi(t)\wedge\phi^{\prime}(s)=\phi^{\prime}(t)\end{gathered}

We proceed by proving both directions of the bidirectional separately by contradiction.

(⇒)(\bm{\Rightarrow})

It is given that

 ∃δ∈Πδ​(ϕ,M):δ∉Πδ​(ϕ′,M)\exists\delta\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,{M}):\delta\notin\Pi_{\delta}(\phi^  (35)
 {\prime},{M})

which can be expanded to

 ∃δ∈Πδ(ϕ,M):∀δ′∈Πδ(ϕ′,M):∃s∈𝒮:δ(⋅∣s)≠δ′(⋅∣s)\exists\delta\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,{M}):\forall\
 delta^{\prime}\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi^{\prime},{M}):\exists s\in\mathcal{S}:\delta(\cdot\mid s)  (36)
 \neq\delta^{\prime}(\cdot\mid s)

We prove the forward implication by contradiction. Assume that

 ∄s,t∈𝒮:ϕ(s)≠ϕ(t)∧ϕ′(s)=ϕ′(t)\nexists s,t\in\mathcal{S}:\phi(s)\neq\phi(t)\wedge\phi^{\prime}  (37)
 (s)=\phi^{\prime}(t)

This can be rewritten as

 ∀s,t∈𝒮:ϕ′​(s)=ϕ′​(t)⇒ϕ⁡(s)=ϕ⁡(t)\forall s,t\in\mathcal{S}:\phi^{\prime}(s)=\phi^{\prime}(t)\  (38)
 Rightarrow\phi(s)=\phi(t)

The contrapositive yields a more useful perspective:

 ∀s,t∈𝒮:ϕ⁡(s)≠ϕ⁡(t)⇒ϕ′​(s)≠ϕ′​(t)\forall s,t\in\mathcal{S}:\phi(s)\neq\phi(t)\Rightarrow\phi^  (39)
 {\prime}(s)\neq\phi^{\prime}(t)

In other words, for every two states s,t∈𝒮s,t\in\mathcal{S} that ϕ\phi maps to different abstract
states, ϕ′\phi^{\prime} will also map them to different states. Every distinction that ϕ\phi makes,
ϕ′\phi^{\prime} also makes. This means that for every abstract policy πϕ\pi_{\phi}, there exists an
equivalent abstract policy πϕ′\pi_{\phi^{\prime}} such that:

 πϕ(⋅∣s)=πϕ′(⋅∣s)∧πϕ(⋅∣t)=πϕ′(⋅∣t)\pi_{\phi}(\cdot\mid s)=\pi_{\phi^{\prime}}(\cdot\mid s)\;\  (40)
 wedge\;\pi_{\phi}(\cdot\mid t)=\pi_{\phi^{\prime}}(\cdot\mid t)

By definition of derived policies, for every derived policy δϕ∈Πδ​(ϕ,M)\delta_{\phi}\in\Pi_{\delta}
(\phi,{M}), there is a matching derived policy δϕ′∈Πδ​(ϕ′,M)\delta_{\phi^{\prime}}\in\Pi_{\delta}(\
phi^{\prime},{M}) such that

 δϕ(⋅∣s)=δϕ′(⋅∣s)∧δϕ(⋅∣t)=δϕ′(⋅∣t)\delta_{\phi}(\cdot\mid s)=\delta_{\phi^{\prime}}(\cdot\mid  (41)
 s)\;\wedge\;\delta_{\phi}(\cdot\mid t)=\delta_{\phi^{\prime}}(\cdot\mid t)

This is in contradiction with the assumption (36) that there exists at least one derived policy 
δϕ∈Πδ​(ϕ,M)\delta_{\phi}\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,{M}) that does not have an equivalent derived policy 
δϕ′∈Πδ​(ϕ′,M)\delta_{\phi^{\prime}}\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi^{\prime},{M}). The contradiction proves the
forward implication.

(⇐)(\bm{\Leftarrow})

It is given that

 ∃s,t∈𝒮:ϕ⁡(s)≠ϕ⁡(t)∧ϕ′​(s)=ϕ′​(t)\begin{gathered}\exists s,t\in\mathcal{S}:\phi(s)\neq\phi(t)  (42)
 \wedge\phi^{\prime}(s)=\phi^{\prime}(t)\end{gathered}

To prove the reverse implication, we proceed by contradiction. Assume that

 ∀δ∈Πδ(ϕ,M):∃δ′∈Πδ(ϕ′,M):∀s∈𝒮:δ(⋅∣s)=δ′(⋅∣s)\forall\delta\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,{M}):\exists\
 delta^{\prime}\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi^{\prime},{M}):\forall s\in\mathcal{S}:\delta(\cdot\mid s)  (43)
 =\delta^{\prime}(\cdot\mid s)

In other words, for each derived policy δϕ∈Πδ​(ϕ,M)\delta_{\phi}\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,{M}) there
exists an equivalent derived policy δϕ′∈Πδ​(ϕ′,M)\delta_{\phi^{\prime}}\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi^{\
prime},{M}).

From (42) we know that there exists an abstract policy which assigns different distributions over
actions to abstract states ϕ⁡(s)\phi(s) and ϕ⁡(t)\phi(t): πϕ(⋅∣ϕ(s))≠πϕ(⋅∣ϕ(t))\pi_{\phi}(\cdot\mid
\phi(s))\neq\pi_{\phi}(\cdot\mid\phi(t)). To contrast, because ϕ′​(s)=ϕ′​(t)\phi^{\prime}(s)=\phi^
{\prime}(t) there can be no πϕ′\pi_{\phi^{\prime}} such that πϕ′(⋅∣ϕ′(s))≠πϕ′(⋅∣ϕ′(t))\pi_{\phi^{\
prime}}(\cdot\mid\phi^{\prime}(s))\neq\pi_{\phi^{\prime}}(\cdot\mid\phi^{\prime}(t)). By the
definition of derived policies, we have

 ∃δ∈Πδ​(ϕ,M)\displaystyle\exists\delta\in\Pi_ :δ(⋅∣s)≠δ(⋅∣t)\displaystyle:\delta(\cdot\mid s)  (44)
 {\delta}(\phi,{M})                           \neq\delta(\cdot\mid t)
 ∀δ′∈Πδ​(ϕ′,M)\displaystyle\forall\delta^{\   :δ′(⋅∣s)=δ′(⋅∣t)\displaystyle:\delta^{\prime}(\  (45)
 prime}\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi^{\prime},{M})     cdot\mid s)=\delta^{\prime}(\cdot\mid t)

That is, it is possible that δ′(⋅∣s)=δ(⋅∣s)\delta^{\prime}(\cdot\mid s)=\delta(\cdot\mid s) or δ′
(⋅∣t)=δ(⋅∣t)\delta^{\prime}(\cdot\mid t)=\delta(\cdot\mid t), but never both. This contradicts the
assumption in (43), proving the reverse implication and completing the proof for the biconditional
in (7).

∎

Theorem 2.3.

Let ϕ:𝒮↦Φ\phi:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\Phi and ϕ′:𝒮↦Φ′\phi^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\Phi^{\prime} be
two state abstractions such that ϕ\phi has strictly higher transfer value than ϕ′\phi^{\prime} for
some MDP M≐⟨𝒮,𝒜,p,r⟩,γ{M}\mathrel{\doteq}\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p,r\rangle,\gamma. Then
there must exist two states s,t∈𝒮s,t\in\mathcal{S} such that ϕ′\phi^{\prime} does not distinguish
between them while ϕ\phi does, and there exists a derived policy for ϕ\phi that takes advantage of
this distinction by controlling ss and tt differently in a way that no derived policy for ϕ′\phi^{\
prime} can do. By giving up representational capacity and increasing potential for transfer, ϕ′\phi
^{\prime} sacrifices transfer value. Formally:

 vϕ​(M)>vϕ′​(M)\displaystyle v_{\phi}({M})>v_{\phi^{\prime}}({M})
 ⇓\displaystyle\Downarrow
 ∃s,t∈𝒮:\displaystyle\exists s,t\in\mathcal{S}:
 1.ϕ⁡(s)≠ϕ⁡(t)2.ϕ′​(s)=ϕ′​(t)3.∃δ∈Πδ(ϕ,M):δ(⋅∣s)≠δ(⋅∣t)4.∀δ′∈Πδ(ϕ′,M):δ′(⋅∣s)=δ′(⋅∣t)\displaystyle
 \begin{aligned} &1.\;\phi(s)\neq\phi(t)\\ &2.\;\phi^{\prime}(s)=\phi^{\prime}(t)\\ &3.\;\exists\
 delta\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,{M}):\delta(\cdot\mid s)\neq\delta(\cdot\mid t)\\ &4.\;\forall\delta^{\
 prime}\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi^{\prime},{M}):\delta^{\prime}(\cdot\mid s)=\delta^{\prime}(\cdot\mid
 t)\end{aligned}

Proof (2.3).

Let M=⟨𝒮,𝒜,p,r⟩{M}=\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p,r\rangle be an MDP and ϕ:𝒮↦Φ\phi:\mathcal{S}\
mapsto\Phi and ϕ′:𝒮↦Φ′\phi^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\Phi^{\prime} be two state abstractions. In
addition we have:

 ∃δ∈Πδ​(ϕ,M):∀δ′∈Πδ​(ϕ′,M):vδ>vδ′\exists\delta\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,{M}):\forall\delta^{\       (46)
 prime}\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi^{\prime},{M}):v_{\delta}>v_{\delta^{\prime}}

For simplicity we will assume deterministic policies in this proof. Instead of distributions over
actions, a policy π:𝒮↦𝒜\pi:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\mathcal{A} simple maps a state to an action. The
extension to stochastic policies is straightforward.

First, we show that there must exist a state s∈𝒮s\in\mathcal{S} for which there is a derived policy
for ϕ\phi that takes a different action from every derived policy for ϕ′\phi^{\prime}. By (46) and
the definition for inequality of value functions we have ∃δ∈Πδ​(ϕ,M):∀δ′∈Πδ​(ϕ′,M):∃s∈𝒮:\exists\
delta\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,{M}):\forall\delta^{\prime}\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi^{\prime},{M}):\exists s\
in\mathcal{S}:

 vδ​(s)>vδ′​(s)⇔r(s,δ(s))+𝐸s′∼p⁡(s,δ⁡(s))[γvδ(s′)]>r(s,δ′(s))+𝐸s′∼p⁡(s,δ′​(s))[γvδ′(s′)]v_{\
 delta}(s)>v_{\delta^{\prime}}(s)\iff\\ r(s,\delta(s))+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s^{\prime}\sim p
 (s,\delta(s))}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\gamma v_{\delta}(s^{\prime})}}\right]>r(s,\delta  (47)
 ^{\prime}(s))+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s^{\prime}\sim p(s,\delta^{\prime}(s))}\mathopen{}\
 mathclose{{\left[\gamma v_{\delta^{\prime}}(s^{\prime})}}\right]\qquad

If δ​(s)=δ′​(s)\delta(s)=\delta^{\prime}(s), the inequality in (47) can only hold if

 𝐸s′∼p⁡(s,δ⁡(s))[γvδ(s′)]>𝐸s′∼p⁡(s,δ′​(s))[γvδ′(s′)]\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s^{\
 prime}\sim p(s,\delta(s))}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\gamma v_{\delta}(s^{\prime})}}\       (48)
 right]>\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s^{\prime}\sim p(s,\delta^{\prime}(s))}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\
 left[\gamma v_{\delta^{\prime}}(s^{\prime})}}\right]

Hence, either the actions at ss are different or inequality (48) also holds. The inequality (48)
can only hold if there is some other state s†s^{\dagger} for which the inequality in (47) holds by
means of δ⁡(s†)≠δ′​(s†)\delta(s^{\dagger})\neq\delta^{\prime}(s^{\dagger}) or that there is some
other state, s†⁣†s^{\dagger\dagger}, reachable in one step from s†s^{\dagger}, for which inequality
(47) also holds. By a process of induction, if Equation (47) holds for ss there must exist some
state, reachable from ss in a finite number of steps under δ\delta (and δ′\delta^{\prime}) for
which the actions under these two policies differ. In short, there exists a state ss where δ⁡(s)
≠δ′​(s)\delta(s)\neq\delta^{\prime}(s). We have:

 ∃δ∈Πδ​(ϕ,M):∀δ′∈Πδ​(ϕ′,M):∃s∈𝒮:δ⁡(s)≠δ′​(s)⇕∃δ∈Πδ(ϕ,M):∄δ′∈Πδ(ϕ′,M):∀s∈𝒮:δ(s)=δ′(s)⇕∃δ∈Πδ
 (ϕ,M):∄δ′∈Πδ(ϕ′,M):δ=δ′⇕∃δ∈Πδ​(ϕ,M):δ∉Πδ​(ϕ′,M)\begin{gathered}\exists\delta\in\Pi_{\delta}
 (\phi,{M}):\forall\delta^{\prime}\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi^{\prime},{M}):\exists s\in\mathcal{S}:
 \delta(s)\neq\delta^{\prime}(s)\\ \Updownarrow\\ \exists\delta\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,{M}):\     (49)
 nexists\delta^{\prime}\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi^{\prime},{M}):\forall s\in\mathcal{S}:\delta(s)=\
 delta^{\prime}(s)\\ \Updownarrow\\ \exists\delta\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,{M}):\nexists\delta^{\
 prime}\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi^{\prime},{M}):\delta=\delta^{\prime}\\ \Updownarrow\\ \exists\
 delta\in\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,{M}):\delta\notin\Pi_{\delta}(\phi^{\prime},{M})\end{gathered}

By Proposition 2.2 we now have that there do exists at least two states s,t∈𝒮s,t\in\mathcal{S} such
that

 ϕ⁡(s)≠ϕ⁡(t)∧ϕ′​(s)=ϕ′​(t)\phi(s)\neq\phi(t)\wedge\phi^{\prime}(s)=\phi^{\prime}(t)  (50)

This proves implications (1) and (2) of the theorem. By repeated invocation of Proposition 2.1,
implications (3) and (4) follow naturally from implications (1) and (2) respectively.

∎

Theorem 2.4.

Let ϕ:𝒮↦Φ\phi:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\Phi and ϕ′:𝒮↦Φ′\phi^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\Phi^{\prime} be
two state abstractions such that ϕ\phi has strictly higher transfer value than ϕ′\phi^{\prime} for
some MDP M{M}. Then ϕ′\phi^{\prime} can both be no finer than ϕ\phi and no more control granular
than ϕ\phi. Formally:

 vϕ​(M)>vϕ′​(M)⟹ϕ′⋡ϕ∧Πδ​(ϕ,M)⊈Πδ​(ϕ′,M)v_{\phi}({M})>v_{\phi^{\prime}}({M})\Longrightarrow\
 phi^{\prime}\not\succeq\phi\;\wedge\;\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,{M})\not\subseteq\Pi_{\delta}(\phi^{\  (51)
 prime},{M})

The following also holds. If ϕ\phi is coarser and less control granular than ϕ′\phi^{\prime}, it is
impossible for ϕ\phi to have a higher transfer value than ϕ′\phi^{\prime}. Formally:

 ϕ′⪰ϕ∧Πδ​(ϕ,M)⊆Πδ​(ϕ′,M)⟹vϕ​(M)≯vϕ′​(M)\phi^{\prime}\succeq\phi\;\wedge\;\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,
 {M})\subseteq\Pi_{\delta}(\phi^{\prime},{M})\Longrightarrow v_{\phi}({M})\not>v_{\phi^{\      (52)
 prime}}({M})

Proof (2.4).

This corollary is the result of a weakening of Theorem 2.3. The first result (51) is a direct
result of the application of the definitions of coarseness and control granularity.

For the second result, consider the contrapositive of (51):

 ϕ′⪰ϕ∨Πδ​(ϕ,M)⊆Πδ​(ϕ′,M)⟹vϕ​(M)≯vϕ′​(M)\phi^{\prime}\succeq\phi\;\vee\;\Pi_{\delta}(\phi,{M})
 \subseteq\Pi_{\delta}(\phi^{\prime},{M})\Longrightarrow v_{\phi}({M})\not>v_{\phi^{\prime}}   (53)
 ({M})

Application of Proposition 2.2 turns the disjunction in (53) into a conjunction, arriving at (52).

∎

Theorem 3.2.

Given two MDPs Mα=⟨𝒮α,𝒜,pα,rα⟩{M}_{\alpha}=\langle\mathcal{S}_{\alpha},\mathcal{A},p_{\alpha},r_{\
alpha}\rangle and Mβ=⟨𝒮β,𝒜,pβ,rβ⟩{M}_{\beta}=\langle\mathcal{S}_{\beta},\mathcal{A},p_{\beta},r_{\
beta}\rangle, two states sα∈𝒮α,sβ∈𝒮βs_{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}},s_{\beta}\in{\mathcal{S}_{\
beta}}, and an action sequence (a1,…,an−1,an)(a_{1},\dots,a_{n-1},a_{n}) such that

 ⟨σ⟩α​(sα,(a1,…,an−1,an))=⟨σ⟩β​(sβ,(a1,…,an−1,an)){\langle\sigma\rangle_{\alpha}}(s_{\alpha},(a_
 {1},\dots,a_{n-1},a_{n}))={\langle\sigma\rangle_{\beta}}(s_{\beta},(a_{1},\dots,a_{n-1},a_{n}))

Then it also holds that

 ⟨σ⟩α​(sα,(a1,…,an−1))=⟨σ⟩β​(sβ,(a1,…,an−1)){\langle\sigma\rangle_{\alpha}}(s_{\alpha},(a_{1},\
 dots,a_{n-1}))={\langle\sigma\rangle_{\beta}}(s_{\beta},(a_{1},\dots,a_{n-1}))

In other words, if two outcome sequence tests for sαs_{\alpha} and sβs_{\beta} are the same for
some length-nn action sequence, then the two outcome sequence tests for sαs_{\alpha} and sβs_{\
beta} constructed from the action subsequence of length n−1n-1 starting from a1a_{1} are also
equal.

Proof (3.2).

This follows directly from Proposition 3.1. If two sequences are equal, then any matching
subsequences are also equal. Given that ⟨σ⟩​(s,(a1,…,an−1))\langle\sigma\rangle(s,(a_{1},\dots,a_
{n-1})) and ⟨σ⟩​(s′,(a1,…,an−1))\langle\sigma\rangle(s^{\prime},(a_{1},\dots,a_{n-1})) are
subsequences of ⟨σ⟩​(s,(a1,…,an−1,an))\langle\sigma\rangle(s,(a_{1},\dots,a_{n-1},a_{n})) and ⟨σ⟩​
(s′,(a1,…,an−1,an))\langle\sigma\rangle(s^{\prime},(a_{1},\dots,a_{n-1},a_{n})) respectively, if
the latter are equal then so must the former be.

∎

Theorem 3.1.

Let M=⟨𝒮,𝒜,p,r⟩{M}=\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p,r\rangle be an MDP with outcome function σ\
sigma. The expected outcome sequence for state s∈𝒮s\in\mathcal{S} and action sequence ⟨a⟩=(a1…an)

{i}) for ii ranging from 11 to nn. Formally:

 ⟨σ⟩(s,⟨a⟩)=(𝐸p[σ(St,At,St+1)∣St=s,At=a1],…,𝐸p[σ(St+n−1,At+n−1,St+n)∣St=s,At=a1,…,At+n−1=an])
 {\langle\sigma\rangle}(s,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle a}}\right\rangle_{)}=\big(\
 mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sigma(S_{t},A_{t},S_{t+1})\mid S_{t}=     (54)
 s,A_{t}=a_{1}}}\right],\dots,\\ \mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sigma
 (S_{t+n-1},A_{t+n-1},S_{t+n})\mid S_{t}=s,A_{t}=a_{1},\dots,A_{t+n-1}=a_{n}}}\right]\big)

 sigma}(\sigma_{i}\mid s,a,s^{\prime})(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n})d(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_  (55)
 {n})

Since c⁡(σ1,…,σn)=(c​σ1,…,c​σn)c(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n})=(c\sigma_{1},\dots,c\sigma_{n}) and
the integral of sequences in Eq. (16) is a sequence of integrals, we can look at each element of
the resulting sequence in turn. Consider the element in the resulting sequence at position ii, with
1≤i≤n1\leq i\leq n:

 {1},...,\sigma_{n})

mathclose{{\left\langle a}}\right\rangle_{)} in full, this becomes:

 ∫Σ∑st+1,…,st+n∈𝒮np⁡(st+1∣st,a1)​…​p​(st+n∣st+n−1,an)pσ​(σ1∣st,a1,st+1)​…pσ​(σn∣st+n−1,an,st+n)

 mid s_{t},a_{1})\dots p(s_{t+n}\mid s_{t+n-1},a_{n})\\ \quad p_{\sigma}(\sigma_{1}\mid s_{t},a_
 {1},s_{t+1})\dots&p_{\sigma}(\sigma_{n}\mid s_{t+n-1},a_{n},s_{t+n})\sigma_{i}d^{n}(\sigma_

Since pσp_{\sigma} is a probability density function conditioned on a transition (s,a,s′)(s,a,s^{\

 (st+i+1∣st+i,ai+1)​…​p​(st+n∣st+n−1,an)\displaystyle\begin{split}&=\sum_{s_{t+1},...,s_{t+i}\in\

This is exactly the expected value of the outcomes starting from state sts_{t} after actions 
a1,…,aia_{1},\dots,a_{i}, i.e. 𝐸p[σ(St+i−1,At+i,St+i)∣St=st,At=a1,…,At+i=ai]\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{p}
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sigma(S_{t+i-1},A_{t+i},S_{t+i})\mid S_{t}=s_{t},A_{t}=a_{1},\dots,A_
{t+i}=a_{i}}}\right]. Putting this together for all ii such that 1≤i≤n1\leq i\leq n results in the
sequence of expectations in Eq. (54), the object of this proof.

∎

Theorem 3.5.

Let Mα=⟨𝒮α,𝒜,pα,rα,γ⟩{M}_{\alpha}=\langle\mathcal{S}_{\alpha},\mathcal{A},p_{\alpha},r_{\alpha},\
gamma\rangle and Mβ=⟨𝒮β,𝒜,pβ,rβ,γ⟩{M}_{\beta}=\langle\mathcal{S}_{\beta},\mathcal{A},p_{\beta},r_{\
beta},\gamma\rangle be two MDPs with respective outcome functions σα\sigma_{\alpha} and σβ\sigma_{\
beta} and reward weightings wrαw_{r_{\alpha}} and wrβw_{r_{\beta}} such that rα=σα⊤​wrαr_{\alpha}=\
sigma_{\alpha}^{\top}w_{r_{\alpha}} and rβ=σβ⊤​wrβr_{\beta}=\sigma_{\beta}^{\top}w_{r_{\beta}}, and
let ϕα:𝒮α↦Φα\phi_{\alpha}:\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\mapsto\Phi_{\alpha} and ϕβ:𝒮β↦Φβ\phi_{\beta}:\
mathcal{S}_{\beta}\mapsto\Phi_{\beta} be two outcome equivalent state abstractions. Then all
abstract states in Φα{\Phi_{\alpha}} are outcome equivalent with their ground states with respect
to any abstract MDP Mϕα=⟨Φα,𝒜,pϕα,rϕβ,γ⟩{M}_{\phi_{\alpha}}=\langle{\Phi_{\alpha}},\mathcal{A},p_{\
phi_{\alpha}},r_{\phi_{\beta}},\gamma\rangle created from Mα{{M}_{\alpha}} and ϕα{\phi_{\alpha}}
with outcome function σϕα\sigma_{\phi_{\alpha}} and reward weighting wrϕαw_{r_{\phi_{\alpha}}} such
that rϕα=σϕα⊤​wrϕαr_{\phi_{\alpha}}=\sigma_{\phi_{\alpha}}^{\top}w_{r_{\phi_{\alpha}}}, given that
the reward weighting for that abstract MDP is the same as those of the ground MDPs, i.e. wrϕα=wrβ=
wrαw_{r_{\phi_{\alpha}}}=w_{r_{\beta}}=w_{r_{\alpha}}. Formally, ∀ϕ¯∈Φα,∀sβ∈ϕβ−1​(ϕ¯)\forall\bar{\
phi}\in{\Phi_{\alpha}},\forall s_{\beta}\in{\phi_{\beta}}^{-1}(\bar{\phi}):

 ⟨σ⟩ϕα​(ϕ¯,𝒜∞)=⟨σ⟩β​(sβ,𝒜∞){\langle\sigma\rangle}_{\phi_{\alpha}}(\bar{\phi},\mathcal{A}^{\    (57)
 infty})={\langle\sigma\rangle_{\beta}}(s_{\beta},\mathcal{A}^{\infty})

Proof (3.5).

For this proof, we assume for simplicity that the abstract state set Φα{\Phi_{\alpha}} is finite.
We proceed by a kind of backward induction, as will become clearer in the induction hypothesis. For
the hypothesis we will assume that the ‘next’ abstract states are outcome equivalent with their
ground states.

Base case

Since this is a proof by backward induction, we start at the end. The base case is a final
absorbing state ϕ¯∈Φα\bar{\phi}\in{\Phi_{\alpha}}. All ground states sβ∈ϕ¯s_{\beta}\in\bar{\phi}
must also be final. Just like there can be no reward past the final state, there can be no outcomes
past the final state either. Since both ϕ¯\bar{\phi} and all sβ∈ϕ¯s_{\beta}\in\bar{\phi} are final
states, they must be outcome equivalent.

Induction hypothesis

We assume that all ‘next’ abstract states ϕ¯t+1∈Φα\bar{\phi}_{t+1}\in{\Phi_{\alpha}} are outcome
equivalent with their ground states. Let Φ¯t\bar{\Phi}_{t} be the random variable describing the
abstract state at time tt. Then it holds that for all states st+1∈ϕβ−1​(ϕ¯t+1)s_{t+1}\in{\phi_{\
beta}^{-1}}(\bar{\phi}_{t+1}): ∀n∈N1\forall n\in\mathbb{N}_{1}: ∀(a2,…,an)∈𝒜n−1\forall(a_{2},\
dots,a_{n})\in\mathcal{A}^{n-1}:

 𝐸pϕα[σ(Φ¯t+n−1,At+n,Φ¯t+n)∣Φ¯t+1=ϕ¯t+1,At+2=a2,…,At+n=an]=𝐸pβ[σ(St+n−1,At+n−1,St+n)∣St+1=
 st+1,At+2=a2,…,At+n=an]\begin{split}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{p_{\phi_{\alpha}}}\mathopen{}\
 mathclose{{\left[\sigma(\bar{\Phi}_{t+n-1},A_{t+n},\bar{\Phi}_{t+n})\mid\bar{\Phi}_{t+1}=\    (58)
 bar{\phi}_{t+1},A_{t+2}=a_{2},\dots,A_{t+n}=a_{n}}}\right]\\ =\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{p_{\
 beta}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sigma(S_{t+n-1},A_{t+n-1},S_{t+n})\mid S_{t+1}=s_
 {t+1},A_{t+2}=a_{2},\dots,A_{t+n}=a_{n}}}\right]\end{split}

Inductive step

We now take a step ‘backward’ and consider the abstract state ϕ¯t\bar{\phi}_{t}, direct predecessor
to states ϕ¯t+1\bar{\phi}_{t+1}. We need to show that ϕ¯t\bar{\phi}_{t} is outcome equivalent with
its ground states.

First we consider the single-step expected outcomes separately for any single action a∈𝒜a\in\
mathcal{A}. From the construction of the abstract reward dynamics rϕαr_{\phi_{\alpha}} we can
derive two identities. The first is based on the definition of the expected outcomes.

 rϕα​(ϕ¯t,a)\           =∑ϕ¯t+1∈Φαpϕα​(ϕ¯t+1∣ϕ¯,a)​σ​(ϕ¯t,a,ϕ¯t+1)⊤​wrϕα\displaystyle=\sum_{\

 =∑st∈ϕβ−1​(ϕ¯t)wϕα(st)𝐸pβ[σ(St+n−1,At+n−1,St+n)∣St=st,At+2=a2,…,At+n=an]\displaystyle\begin
 {split}=&\sum_{s_{t}\in{\phi_{\beta}^{-1}}(\bar{\phi}_{t})}w_{{\phi_{\alpha}}}(s_{t})\mathop{\
 mathbb{E}}_{p_{\beta}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sigma(S_{t+n-1},A_{t+n-1},S_{t+n})\mid S_{t}=
 s_{t},A_{t+2}=a_{2},\dots,A_{t+n}=a_{n}}}\right]\end{split}

Finally, since we have that all states st∈ϕβ−1​(ϕ¯t)s_{t}\in{\phi_{\beta}^{-1}}(\bar{\phi}_{t}) are
outcome equivalent and that for any ϕ¯\bar{\phi} it holds that ∑s∈ϕβ−1​(ϕ¯)wϕα​(s)=1\sum_{s\in{\
phi_{\beta}^{-1}}(\bar{\phi})}w_{{\phi_{\alpha}}}(s)=1, this further simplifies to

 𝐸pβ[σ(St+n−1,At+n−1,St+n)∣St=st,At+2=a2,…,At+n=an]\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{p_{\beta}}\mathopen{}
 \mathclose{{\left[\sigma(S_{t+n-1},A_{t+n-1},S_{t+n})\mid S_{t}=s_{t},A_{t+2}=a_{2},\dots,A_  (62)
 {t+n}=a_{n}}}\right]

This proves the equality between Equations (61) and (62), completing the inductive step. We
conclude that any abstract state ϕ¯∈Φα\bar{\phi}\in{\Phi_{\alpha}} is outcome equivalent to all of
its ground states with respect to ϕβ{\phi_{\beta}}.

∎

Lemma A.1.

Let M=⟨𝒮,𝒜,p,r,γ⟩{M}=\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p,r,\gamma\rangle be a plannable MDP with
outcome function σ\sigma and reward weighting wrw_{r} such that r=σ⊤​wrr=\sigma^{\top}w_{r}, and
let ϕ:𝒮↦Φ\phi:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\Phi be an outcome equivalent state abstraction. Then any abstract
policy πϕ\pi_{\phi} for an abstract MDP Mϕ=⟨Φ,𝒜,pϕ,rϕ,γ⟩{M}_{\phi}=\langle\Phi,\mathcal{A},p_{\

reward weighting wrϕw_{r_{\phi}} such that rϕ=σϕ⊤​wrϕr_{\phi}=\sigma_{\phi}^{\top}w_{r_{\phi}} and
with the same reward weighting, i.e. wrϕ=wrw_{r_{\phi}}=w_{r}, is derived value–compatible.

Lemma A.2.

Let M=⟨𝒮,𝒜,p,r,γ⟩{M}=\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p,r,\gamma\rangle be a plannable MDP with
outcome function σ\sigma and reward weighting wrw_{r} such that r=σ⊤​wrr=\sigma^{\top}w_{r}, and
let ϕ:𝒮↦Φ\phi:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\Phi be an outcome equivalent state abstraction. Then any abstract
MDP Mϕ=⟨Φ,𝒜,pϕ,rϕ,γ⟩{M}_{\phi}=\langle\Phi,\mathcal{A},p_{\phi},r_{\phi},\gamma\rangle created from
M{M} and ϕ\phi with outcome function σϕ\sigma_{\phi} and reward weighting wrϕw_{r_{\phi}} such that
rϕ=σϕ⊤​wrϕr_{\phi}=\sigma_{\phi}^{\top}w_{r_{\phi}} and with the same reward weighting, i.e. wrϕ=
wrw_{r_{\phi}}=w_{r}, is also plannable.

Proof (A.2).

Since M{M} is plannable, we know that for all states s∈𝒮s\in\mathcal{S} there exists an action
sequence (a1,…,an)∈𝒜n(a_{1},...,a_{n})\in\mathcal{A}^{n} with nn going to ∞\infty in the limit such
that the following holds:

 vπ​(s)\        =𝐸p[∑nk=1γk−1Rt+k|St=s,At=a1,…,At+n−1=an]\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{p}\
 displaystyle   mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum^{n}_{k=1}\gamma^{k-1}R_{t+k}\mathrel{}\middle|\
 v_{\pi}(s)     mathrel{}S_{t}=s,A_{t}=a_{1},...,A_{t+n-1}=a_{n}}}\right]

From Proposition 3.5, we know that all abstract states ϕ¯∈Φ\bar{\phi}\in\Phi are outcome equivalent
with their ground states s∈ϕ¯s\in\bar{\phi}. Because ϕ¯\bar{\phi} and ss are outcome equivalent,
the expected outcomes are also the same for any subsequence of actions (Proposition 3.1). Their γ\
gamma-discounted sums must also be, and so we can rewrite the above equality in terms of abstract
outcomes, and, consequently, in terms of abstract rewards. Let the following shorthand expectations
be conditioned on the action sequence such that At=a1,…,At+n−1=anA_{t}=a_{1},\dots,A_{t+n-1}=a_{n}:

 vπ​(s)\      =𝐸p[∑nk=1γk−1σ(St+k−1,At+k−1,St+k)|St=s]⊤wr\displaystyle=\mathop
 displaystyle {\mathbb{E}}_{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum^{n}_{k=1}\gamma
 v_{\pi}(s)   ^{k-1}\sigma(S_{t+k-1},A_{t+k-1},S_{t+k})\mathrel{}\middle|\
              mathrel{}S_{t}=s}}\right]^{\top}w_{r}
              =𝐸p[∑nk=1γk−1σ(St+k−1,At+k−1,St+k)|St=s]⊤wrϕ\displaystyle=\mathop  wrϕ=wr\
              {\mathbb{E}}_{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum^{n}_{k=1}\gamma  displaystyle w_
              ^{k-1}\sigma(S_{t+k-1},A_{t+k-1},S_{t+k})\mathrel{}\middle|\       {r_{\phi}}=w_{r}
              mathrel{}S_{t}=s}}\right]^{\top}w_{r_{\phi}}
              =𝐸pϕ[∑nk=1γk−1σϕ(Φ¯t+n−1,At+n−1,Φ¯t+n)|Φ¯t=ϕ(s)]⊤wrϕ\displaystyle
              =\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{p_{\phi}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum^
              {n}_{k=1}\gamma^{k-1}\sigma_{\phi}(\bar{\Phi}_{t+n-1},A_{t+n-1},\  (Proposition 3.1)
              bar{\Phi}_{t+n})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\bar{\Phi}_{t}=\phi
              (s)}}\right]^{\top}w_{r_{\phi}}
              =𝐸pϕ[∑nk=1γk−1Rt+k|Φ¯t=ϕ(s)]\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{p_
              {\phi}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum^{n}_{k=1}\gamma^{k-1}R_
              {t+k}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\bar{\Phi}_{t}=\phi(s)}}\right]

We have that the value function for every ground policy π\pi can be expressed as the expected
reward given an abstract state and a sequence of actions. Some of these ground policies are derived
from abstract policies. Lemma A.1 states that all abstract policies are derived value–compatible.
Putting this together, we have

 vπ(s)=𝐸pϕ[∑nk=1γk−1Rt+k|Φ¯t=ϕ(s)]v_{\pi}(s)=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{p_{\phi}}\mathopen{}\mathclose
 {{\left[\sum^{n}_{k=1}\gamma^{k-1}R_{t+k}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\bar{\Phi}_{t}=\phi(s)}}\
 right]

This proves that Mϕ{M}_{\phi} is also plannable because it is constructed from a plannable MDP and
an outcome equivalent state abstraction. ∎

Lemma A.3.

Let M=⟨𝒮,𝒜,p,r,γ⟩{M}=\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p,r,\gamma\rangle be a plannable MDP and let 
ϕ:𝒮↦Φ\phi:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\Phi be an outcome equivalent state abstraction. Then it holds that all
states in the same equivalence class under ϕ\phi have the same value for any policy. Formally, 
∀ϕ¯∈Φ\forall\bar{\phi}\in\Phi, ∀s,s′∈ϕ¯\forall s,s^{\prime}\in\bar{\phi} and for any policy π\pi:

 vπ​(s)=vπ​(s′)v_{\pi}(s)=v_{\pi}(s^{\prime})

Proof (A.3).

Consider an abstract state ϕ¯∈Φ\bar{\phi}\in\Phi with two ground states s,s′∈ϕ¯s,s^{\prime}\in\bar
{\phi}. They are outcome equivalent, meaning that for any action sequence (a1,…,an)∈𝒜n(a_{1},...,a_
{n})\in\mathcal{A}^{n} (for any length nn), the expected outcomes are the same when starting from
either ss or s′s^{\prime}.

 {p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sigma(S_{t+n-1},A_{t+n-1},S_{t+n}\mid S_{t}=s)}}\right]=\mathop

 prime})}}\right]

is plannable, it must be that vπ​(s)=vπ​(s′)v_{\pi}(s)=v_{\pi}(s^{\prime}) (by the definition of

Lemma A.4.

Let Mα=⟨𝒮α,𝒜,pα,rα,γ⟩{M}_{\alpha}=\langle\mathcal{S}_{\alpha},\mathcal{A},p_{\alpha},r_{\alpha},\

Theorem 3.6.

Let Mα=⟨𝒮α,𝒜,pα,rα,γ⟩{{M}_{\alpha}}=\langle\mathcal{S}_{\alpha},\mathcal{A},p_{\alpha},r_{\alpha},\
gamma\rangle and Mβ=⟨𝒮β,𝒜,pβ,rβ,γ⟩{{M}_{\beta}}=\langle\mathcal{S}_{\beta},\mathcal{A},p_{\beta},r_
{\beta},\gamma\rangle, be two plannable MDPs with respective outcome functions σα\sigma_{\alpha}
and σβ\sigma_{\beta} and reward weightings wrαw_{r_{\alpha}} and wrβw_{r_{\beta}} such that rα=
σα⊤​wrαr_{\alpha}=\sigma_{\alpha}^{\top}w_{r_{\alpha}} and rβ=σβ⊤​wrβr_{\beta}=\sigma_{\beta}^{\
top}w_{r_{\beta}}, and let ϕα:𝒮α↦Φα\phi_{\alpha}:\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\mapsto\Phi_{\alpha} and 
ϕβ:𝒮β↦Φβ\phi_{\beta}:\mathcal{S}_{\beta}\mapsto\Phi_{\beta} be two outcome equivalent state
abstractions state abstractions. Then any abstract MDP Mϕα=⟨Φα,𝒜,pϕα,rϕβ,γ⟩{M}_{\phi_{\alpha}}=\
langle{\Phi_{\alpha}},\mathcal{A},p_{\phi_{\alpha}},r_{\phi_{\beta}},\gamma\rangle created from Mα
{{M}_{\alpha}} and ϕα{\phi_{\alpha}} with outcome function σϕα\sigma_{\phi_{\alpha}} and reward
weighting wrϕαw_{r_{\phi_{\alpha}}} such that rϕα=σϕα⊤​wrϕαr_{\phi_{\alpha}}=\sigma_{\phi_{\alpha}}
^{\top}w_{r_{\phi_{\alpha}}} and such that wrϕα=wrα=wrβw_{r_{\phi_{\alpha}}}=w_{r_{\alpha}}=w_{r_{\
beta}}, is transfer optimal for Mβ{{M}_{\beta}}.

Proof (3.6).

Let Mα{{M}_{\alpha}} and Mβ{{M}_{\beta}} be two plannable MDPs and let ϕα:𝒮α↦Φα\phi_{\alpha}:\
mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\mapsto\Phi_{\alpha} and ϕβ:𝒮β↦Φβ\phi_{\beta}:\mathcal{S}_{\beta}\mapsto\Phi_{\
beta} be two state abstractions such that Φα∪Φβ{\Phi_{\alpha}}\cup{\Phi_{\beta}}.

In order to prove that the abstract MDP Mϕα{M}_{\phi_{\alpha}} is transfer optimal for Mβ{{M}_{\
beta}}, we need to prove two properties (from the definition of transfer optimality):

 1. 1.
   
    all optimal abstract policies πϕα∗:Φα↦𝒫⁡(𝒜)\pi^{*}_{\phi_{\alpha}}:\Phi_{\alpha}\mapsto\mathcal
    {P}(\mathcal{A}) of Mϕα{M}_{\phi_{\alpha}} are derived value–compatible with respect to Mβ{M}_
    {\beta}; and

 2. 2.
   
    the guaranteed transfer value of Mϕα{M}_{\phi_{\alpha}} for Mβ{M}_{\beta} is maximal (i.e. the
    optimal policy for Mβ{M}_{\beta} can be partially derived from every optimal abstract policy
    for Mϕα{M}_{\phi_{\alpha}}).

First we prove derived value–compatibility for all optimal abstract policies. This is a direct
consequence of Lemma A.4, which states that all abstract policies are derived value–compatible,
which is a stronger property.

For the second part of the proof, we prove that the optimal policy for Mβ{M}_{\beta} can be
partially derived from every optimal abstract policy πϕα\pi_{\phi_{\alpha}} for Mϕα{M}_{\phi_{\
alpha}}. We prove this part by contradiction, by assuming there exists some policy π¯\bar{\pi} that
is not derived from any πϕα\pi_{\phi_{\alpha}} with a higher value than any partially derived
policy δ\delta. We proceed in the same vain as the transfer value–control trade-off theorem
(Theorem 2.3). For it to be possible that π¯\bar{\pi} has a higher value than any derived policy δ\
delta, there must exist two states s,t∈𝒮βs,t\in{\mathcal{S}_{\beta}} such that ϕβ​(s)=ϕβ​(t)∈Φα{\
phi_{\beta}}(s)={\phi_{\beta}}(t)\in{\Phi_{\alpha}}, and π¯(⋅|s)≠π¯(⋅|t)\bar{\pi}(\cdot|s)\neq\bar
{\pi}(\cdot|t). That is, π¯\bar{\pi} has more control leading to an increase in value compared to δ
\delta, i.e. vπ¯​(s)>vπ¯​(t)v_{\bar{\pi}}(s)>v_{\bar{\pi}}(t) and vδ​(s)=vδ​(t)v_{\delta}(s)=v_{\
delta}(t). Lemma A.3 outcome equivalent states in a plannable MDP have the same value for any
policy, i.e. vπ¯​(s)=vπ¯​(t)v_{\bar{\pi}}(s)=v_{\bar{\pi}}(t). This is a direct contradiction and
so we conclude that there is no optimal policy π¯\bar{\pi} that is not a derived policy.

Putting the two properties together, we conclude that Mϕα{M}_{\phi_{\alpha}} is transfer optimal
for Mβ{{M}_{\beta}}.

∎

Appendix B Option Discovery

Most of the below is due to Daniel et al. (2016), albeit reformulated. Major changes are indicated.

Consider a demonstrated trajectory as a sequence of states and actions as experienced by an agent
in some MDP:

 τ=⟨s1,a1,…,sT,aT,sT+1⟩,{\tau=\langle s_{1},a_{1},\dots,s_{T},a_{T},s_{T+1}\rangle},

The goal is to discover a hierarchical agent that employs a high-level policy over a set of options

 Ω={ω|ω=(𝒮ω,πω,βω)}\Omega=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\omega\middle|\omega=(\mathcal{S}_{\
 omega},\pi_{\omega},\beta_{\omega})}}\right\}

that is most likely to give rise to this same trajectory. Since we deal with multiple tasks with
distinct state sets, we assume that for each task there is an associated state representation 
ρ:𝒮↦𝒮ω\rho:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\mathcal{S}_{\omega}. The method outlined here works for any shared
state set 𝒮ω\mathcal{S}_{\omega}, though in our case ρ\rho will always be an Outcome-Predictive
State Representation, so ρ=ϕσ,k\rho=\phi_{\sigma,k} with 𝒮ω=Φσ,k\mathcal{S}_{\omega}=\Phi_{\
sigma,k}. That is, all options operate on the same abstract OPSR.

Different from previous work, we also consider an auxiliary option token ω∅\omega_{\varnothing}
which represents “no option”, giving rise to the extended option set

 Ω+=Ω∪{ω∅}\Omega^{+}=\Omega\cup\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\omega_{\varnothing}{}}}\right\}  (64)

This allows us to write the latent trajectory describing the hierarchical agent’s option occupancy
as

 τL=⟨ω1,…,ωT⟩∈Ω+T{\tau_{L}=\langle\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{T}\rangle\in{\Omega^{+}}^{T}}  (65)

with tokens indicating the active option ωt\omega_{t} at each timestep tt.


Procedure 3 Pseudo-code of hierarchical generative model
s1∼pinit,ω0←ω∅s_{1}\sim p_{\text{init}},\omega_{0}\leftarrow\omega_{\varnothing}
for t←1​…​Tt\leftarrow 1\dots T do
  draw βt∼βωt−1​(ρωt−1​(st))\beta_{t}\sim\beta_{\omega_{t-1}}(\rho_{\omega_{t-1}}(s_{t})) ⊳\
triangleright βω∅\beta_{\omega_{\varnothing}} always returns 11
  if βt\beta_{t} then
   ωt←ω∅\omega_{t}\leftarrow\omega_{\varnothing}{} ⊳\triangleright option terminates
  else
   ωt←ωt−1\omega_{t}\leftarrow\omega_{t-1} ⊳\triangleright option continues   
  if ωt=ω∅\omega_{t}=\omega_{\varnothing}{} then
   draw ωt∼πH(⋅|st)\omega_{t}\sim\pi_{H}(\cdot|s_{t}) ⊳\triangleright Draw new option or ω∅\omega_
{\varnothing}   
  draw at∼πωt(⋅|ρωt(st))a_{t}\sim\pi_{\omega_{t}}(\cdot|\rho_{\omega_{t}}(s_{t}))
  draw st+1∼p(⋅|ρωt(st),at)s_{t+1}\sim p(\cdot|\rho_{\omega_{t}}(s_{t}),a_{t})

We assume that the two trajectories τ\tau and τL\tau_{L} are generated by the generative model
described in Algorithm 3. This allows us to rewrite the probability of a trace as

previously in use:

 Pr⁡(ωt|st,ωt−1)={πH​(ωt|st),ωt−1=ω∅(1−βωt−1​(st))​δωt−1=ωt+βωt−1​(st)​πH​(ωt|st)ωt−1≠ω∅\Pr(\

Both option control and termination policies are parameterized by parameters θω=(θπω,θβω)\theta_{\
omega}=(\theta_{\pi_{\omega}},\theta_{\beta_{\omega}}), and the high-level policy is parameterized
by θπH\theta_{\pi_{H}}. For example, these could be the weights of neural networks. Jointly, we
write θ=(θπH,(θω)ω∈Ω)\theta=(\theta_{\pi_{H}},(\theta_{\omega})_{\omega\in\Omega}).

We now phrase the problem of option discovery as maximizing the log-likelihood of the demonstrated
trajectory.

 θmax=arg⁡maxθ⁡L​[θ;τ]=arg⁡maxθ​log​Prθ⁡(τ)\theta_{\textsc{max}}=\argmax_{\theta}L[\theta;\    (69)
 tau]=\argmax_{\theta}\log\Pr_{\theta}(\tau)

The original Expectation-Gradient algorithm that performs this maximization is due to Daniel et al.
(2016), along with its extension by Fox et al. (2017) to allow for neural networks. These versions
contain a more complicated representation of the latent trajectory which included termination
tokens. This greatly complicates both the representation and the implementation of the algorithm.
Below we present the simpler yet equivalent algorithm that results from removing termination tokens
from the latent trajectory.

Baum-Welch forward-backward algorithm

Equation (76) relies on two posteriors:

 ut​(ω)\displaystyle u_{t}(\      =Pr⁡(ωt=ω|τ)\displaystyle=\Pr({\bbomega}_{t}=\omega|\tau)    (77)
 omega)
 vt​(ω,ω′)\displaystyle v_{t}(\   =Pr⁡(ωt=ω′,ωt−1=ω|τ)\displaystyle=\Pr({\bbomega}_{t}=\omega  (78)
 omega,\omega^{\prime})           ^{\prime},\bbomega_{t-1}=\omega|\tau)

We can model the problem as a Hidden Markov Model (HMM) and make use of a modified version of the
Baum-Welch algorithm to make computing these posteriors computationally feasible. To sketch the
HMM, consider that the observed action at each time step relies only on the state and the latent
active option.

The Baum-Welch algorithm is based on the following observation. We start by rewriting equation (77
).

 displaystyle\Pr  =Pr⁡(τ|ωt)​Pr⁡(ωt)Pr⁡(τ)\displaystyle=\frac{\Pr(\  Bayes’ rule               (79)

                  displaystyle=\frac{\Pr(\tau_{1:t}|\omega_{t})\Pr   displaystyle\tau_{1:t}\   (80)
                  (\tau_{t+1:T})|\omega_{t})\Pr(\omega_{t})}{\Pr(\   perp\tau_{t+1:T}|\omega_
                  tau)}                                              {t}
                  =Pr(τ1:t,ωt)Pr(τt+1:T|ωt)Pr⁡(τ)\displaystyle=\
                  frac{\Pr(\tau_{1:t},\omega_{t})\Pr(\tau_{t+1:T}|\                            (81)
                  omega_{t})}{\Pr(\tau)}

Equation (81) gives us the two main entities of the algorithm.

 αt​(ωt)\displaystyle\alpha_{t}(\omega_  =Pr(τ1:t,ωt)\displaystyle=\Pr(\tau_{1:t},\omega_{t})  (82)

These can be computed efficiently with a recursive forward and backward pass respectively,
following the dynamic programming paradigm. Finally, the marginal and joint posteriors can be
recovered.

 Pr⁡(ωt|τ)\displaystyle\  =z−1​αt​(ωt)​βt​(ωt)\displaystyle=z^{-1}\alpha_{t}(\omega_{t})\      (84)

 Pr⁡(ωt,ωt+1|τ)\          =z−1​αt​(ωt)​βt+1​(ωt+1)​Pr⁡(ωt+1|ωt,st+1)​Pr​(at+1|ωt+1,st+1)\
 displaystyle\Pr(\omega_  displaystyle=z^{-1}\alpha_{t}(\omega_{t})\beta_{t+1}(\omega_{t+1})\  (85)
 {t},\omega_{t+1}|\tau)   Pr(\omega_{t+1}|\omega_{t},s_{t+1})\Pr(a_{t+1}|\omega_{t+1},s_
                          {t+1})

where z=Pr⁡(τ)z=\Pr(\tau) is the normalisation constant first introduced in (81).

Assuming θ\theta fixed, we now have a closed-form and exact expression for the gradient in (76).
Expectation-Gradient simply alternates between solving the expectation in (76) which gives rise to
a gradient. This gradient can now be climbed with a gradient ascent method.
